Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Since the giant heads have appeared in the sky all humanity is in danger, so all Ricks and Mortys from all parallel universes are gathering in groups to find a solution to get rid of them. 

There are n parallel universes participating in this event (n Ricks and n Mortys). I. e. each of n universes has one Rick and one Morty. They're gathering in m groups. Each person can be in many groups and a group can contain an arbitrary number of members.

Ricks and Mortys have registered online in these groups. So, a person can have joined a group more than once (developer of this website hadn't considered this possibility). [Image] 

Summer from universe #1 knows that in each parallel universe (including hers) exactly one of Rick and Morty from that universe is a traitor and is loyal, but no one knows which one. She knows that we are doomed if there's a group such that every member in that group is a traitor (they will plan and destroy the world). 

Summer knows that if there's a possibility that world ends (there's a group where all members are traitors) she should immediately cancel this event. So she wants to know if she should cancel the event. You have to tell her yes if and only if there's at least one scenario (among all 2^{n} possible scenarios, 2 possible scenarios for who a traitor in each universe) such that in that scenario the world will end.


-----Input-----

The first line of input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^4) — number of universes and number of groups respectively.

The next m lines contain the information about the groups. i-th of them first contains an integer k (number of times someone joined i-th group, k > 0) followed by k integers v_{i}, 1, v_{i}, 2, ..., v_{i}, k. If v_{i}, j is negative, it means that Rick from universe number  - v_{i}, j has joined this group and otherwise it means that Morty from universe number v_{i}, j has joined it.

Sum of k for all groups does not exceed 10^4.


-----Output-----

In a single line print the answer to Summer's question. Print "YES" if she should cancel the event and "NO" otherwise.


-----Examples-----
Input
4 2
1 -3
4 -2 3 2 -3

Output
YES

Input
5 2
5 3 -2 1 -1 5
3 -5 2 5

Output
NO

Input
7 2
3 -1 6 7
7 -5 4 2 4 7 -3 4

Output
YES



-----Note-----

In the first sample testcase, 1st group only contains the Rick from universe number 3, so in case he's a traitor, then all members of this group are traitors and so Summer should cancel the event.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. There are `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. People can join groups multiple times (so same person can appear multiple times in a group)
4. The world ends if there exists ANY group where ALL members are traitors
5. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario (assignment of traitors) where the world ends

## Key Insight

The world ends if there's a group where all members are traitors. For a group to have all traitors, we need to find a way to assign traitors such that:
- In the relevant universe, the person who joined is a traitor
- For any universe that appears in the group, we must assign the traitor role to the person who joined (Rick or Morty)

For a group to potentially have all traitors, we need to check if we can assign traitors such that:
- For each person in the group from universe `u`, we assign the traitor role to that person (Rick or Morty)
- The constraint is that in each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor

## Key Observation

For a group to have all traitors in some scenario, we need to find a consistent assignment. This is possible if and only if there's no universe that appears with both Rick and Morty in the same group.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, for a group to have all traitors, we need to assign traitor status to each person in the group such that:
- For each universe, only one person (Rick or Morty) is chosen as traitor
- For every person in the group from universe `u`, that person must be the traitor

This creates a constraint. For a group to be possible (all traitors), we need that for every universe that appears in the group, we can assign the traitor role consistently.

Actually, let me think differently: For a group to potentially have all traitors, we need that in that group, for each universe, we can assign the traitor role to someone who is in the group.

But since in each universe exactly one person is a traitor, if a universe appears in a group with both Rick and Morty, then we could assign either one to be the traitor. So such a group could potentially have all traitors by choosing the traitor appropriately.

Wait, that's not right. If both Rick and Morty from universe `u` appear in a group, we can choose either one to be the traitor. So the group could potentially have all traitors.

If only Rick appears, we need Rick to be the traitor.
If only Morty appears, we need Morty to be the traitor.

The only case where a group CANNOT have all traitors is when a universe has both Rick and Morty present in the group - because we can choose either one to be the traitor, but we need both to be traitors at the same time.

Actually, no - if both Rick and Morty from universe `u` are in the group, then for the group to have all traitors, both must be traitors. But this is impossible because each universe has exactly one traitor.

So: a group has all traitors if and only if in that group, for every universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty appears (or both don't appear).

Wait, let me re-think:

For a group to have all traitors in some scenario:
- For each universe that appears in the group, we need to assign the traitor role to someone in that universe who is in the group
- But we can only assign one traitor per universe

So if a universe appears in the group with both Rick and Morty, it's impossible for both to be traitors (since only one can be). Therefore, such a group cannot have all traitors.

If a universe appears in the group with only Rick, we can have Rick as traitor.
If a universe appears in the group with only Morty, we can have Morty as traitor.

Therefore, a group can potentially have all traitors if and only if no universe appears in the group with both Rick and Morty.

In other words, if there exists any group where a universe has both Rick and Morty present, then that group CANNOT have all traitors.

But if there is a group where some universe has only Rick or only Morty, we CAN have all traitors in that group (by choosing the appropriate one as traitor).

So the answer is "YES" if there exists ANY group that has all traitors in some scenario, which happens when for that group, no universe appears with both Rick and Morty.

Actually, let me correct: the answer is "YES" if there exists at least one group such that in that group, every person is from a universe where we can assign the traitor role.

For a group to have all traitors: for each universe in the group, we need to assign the traitor role to someone from that universe who appears in the group.

This is possible if and only if for each universe that appears in the group, not both Rick and Morty appear in the group. Because if both appear in the group, we'd need both to be traitors, which is impossible.

Therefore, we need to check if there exists ANY group such that no universe has both Rick and Morty in that group.

If yes, then we can have a scenario where that group has all traitors (just assign the traitor role to the person who appears in that group for each universe), so answer is "YES".

If no such group exists (every group has at least one universe with both Rick and Morty), then answer is "NO".

Wait, I think I'm confused. Let me re-read.

We want to know if there exists a scenario where the world ends (some group has all traitors).

So: if we can find ANY group where we can assign traitor statuses such that all members of that group are traitors, then answer is YES.

For a given group, can we assign traitor statuses such that all members are traitors?
- For each universe that appears in this group, we must assign the traitor role to someone from that universe who is in the group.
- This is only possible if for each universe in the group, not both Rick and Morty appear in the group.

Therefore, if for a group, every universe in it has either only Rick or only Morty, then we can make that group have all traitors.

So the algorithm:
1. For each group, check if any universe appears with both Rick and Morty
2. If any group has no universe with both Rick and Morty, then YES (we can create a scenario where that group has all traitors)
3. Otherwise, NO

Wait, actually: if a group has at least one universe with both Rick and Morty, then it's impossible for all members to be traitors (since one universe can't have both traitors).

If a group has no universe with both Rick and Morty, then for each universe in the group, either only Rick or only Morty is present, so we can assign the traitor role to that person.

So we just need to find if there exists ANY group where no universe appears with both Rick and Morty.

If such a group exists, answer is YES.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

found_valid_group = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:k+1]
    
    # Track which universes appear with both Rick and Morty
    universe_has_both = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Negative means Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            # We only care if both Rick and Morty appear from this universe
            # So we need to check if Morty from same universe also appears
            # We'll record that Rick appears from universe
            universe_has_both.add(universe)
        else:
            # Positive means Morty from universe member
            universe = member
            universe_has_both.add(universe)
    
    # Check if any universe appears with both Rick and Morty
    # Actually, we just need to check if there's a universe that appears with both
    # But we don't have the complete set of appearances - we need to know if both appeared
    
    # Let's restructure: for this group, we need to check if there's any universe where both Rick and Morty appear
    # We'll create a set for each universe's presence
    universe_rick = set()
    universe_morty = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            universe = -member
            universe_rick.add(universe)
        else:
            universe = member
            universe_morty.add(universe)
    
    # Check if there's any universe that has both Rick and Morty in this group
    has_both = False
    for universe in universe_rick:
        if universe in universe_morty:
            has_both = True
            break
    
    # If no universe has both, then we can assign traitors so that all members in this group are traitors
    if not has_both:
        found_valid_group = True
        break

if found_valid_group:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. There are `n` universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. There are `m` groups, and each person can join multiple groups (duplicate entries are possible)
4. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario where a group has ALL traitors
5. If such a scenario exists, output "YES", otherwise "NO"

## Key Insight

For a group to have all traitors, every member in that group must be a traitor in their respective universe.

In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor. So for a group to have all traitors:
- If a group contains both Rick from universe i and Morty from universe i, they can't both be traitors (since exactly one of them is traitor in each universe)
- Therefore, if a group contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe, it's impossible for all members to be traitors

## Algorithm

1. For each group, check if it contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe
2. If we find such a case, then it's impossible for all members in that group to be traitors
3. However, we need to check if there exists ANY scenario where some group has all traitors

Wait, let me reconsider. The question is: "is there at least one scenario where the world ends?"

For the world to end, there must exist at least one group where all members are traitors.

So we need to check: for each group, is it possible that all members in that group are traitors?

For a group to have all traitors:
- For each universe, if the group contains both Rick and Morty from that universe, then it's impossible (since exactly one of them is traitor)
- If a group contains only one member from a universe (either Rick or Morty), then we can choose that one to be the traitor
- If a group contains members from different universes, we can make each one in the group a traitor as long as no universe has both its Rick and Morty in the group

So for a group to be possible with all traitors:
- It must not contain both Rick and Morty from the same universe

But wait - we need to be more careful. The condition is: is there ANY scenario where a group has all traitors?

This happens if and only if for some group, we can assign traitors such that every member in that group is a traitor.

For a given group to be possible with all traitors:
- For each universe i, if both Rick and Morty from universe i are in the group, then it's impossible
- If only one of them is in the group, we can make that one the traitor
- If neither is in the group, it doesn't matter

Therefore, if we can find at least one group where no universe has both its Rick and Morty present, then we can make all members of that group traitors.

## Solution Steps

1. Read input
2. For each group, check if it contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe
3. If any group doesn't contain both from the same universe, then "YES" (world ends in some scenario)
4. Otherwise, "NO"

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Check each group
found_possible = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:k+1]
    
    # Track which universes have both Rick and Morty in this group
    has_both_universe = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Negative means Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            # We have Rick from universe
            if universe in has_both_universe:
                # This universe already had Morty in the group
                # So both Rick and Morty from this universe are in the group
                # This makes it impossible for all members to be traitors
                pass
            else:
                # Add this universe to set of universes that have Rick
                has_both_universe.add(universe)
        else:
            # Positive means Morty from universe member
            universe = member
            # We have Morty from universe
            if universe in has_both_universe:
                # This universe already had Rick in the group
                # So both Rick and Morty from this universe are in the group
                # This makes it impossible for all members to be traitors
                pass
            else:
                # Add this universe to set of universes that have Morty
                has_both_universe.add(universe)
    
    # If this group doesn't contain both Rick and Morty from any single universe,
    # then it's possible for all members to be traitors
    # But we need to check if there's ANY universe that has both
    # If there's no universe with both, then we can assign traitors to make all members traitors
    
    # Actually, if no universe has both Rick and Morty in this group,
    # then we can make all members traitors by choosing the traitor appropriately
    
    # Check if there's any universe that has both Rick and Morty
    # We need to check if any universe appears both as a negative and positive value
    
    # Let's reconstruct the set of universes that have both
    universe_with_rikg = set()
    universe_with_morty = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            universe_with_rikg.add(-member)
        else:
            universe_with_morty.add(member)
    
    # Find intersection - universes that have both Rick and Morty
    common_universes = universe_with_rikg & universe_with_morty
    
    # If there are no common universes, then this group can have all traitors
    if len(common_universes) == 0:
        found_possible = True
        break

if found_possible:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Check each group
found_possible = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:k+1]
    
    # Track which universes have both Rick and Morty in this group
    has_both_universe = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Negative means Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            # We have Rick from universe
            if universe in has_both_universe:
                # This universe already had Morty in the group
                # So both Rick and Morty from this universe are in the group
                # This makes it impossible for all members to be traitors
                pass
            else:
                # Add this universe to set of universes that have Rick
                has_both_universe.add(universe)
        else:
            # Positive means Morty from universe member
            universe = member
            # We have Morty from universe
            if universe in has_both_universe:
                # This universe already had Rick in the group
                # So both Rick and Morty from this universe are in the group
                # This makes it impossible for all members to be traitors
                pass
            else:
                # Add this universe to set of universes that have Morty
                has_both_universe.add(universe)
    
    # If this group doesn't contain both Rick and Morty from any single universe,
    # then it's possible for all members to be traitors
    # But we need to check if there's ANY universe that has both
    # If there's no universe with both, then we can assign traitors to make all members traitors
    
    # Actually, if no universe has both Rick and Morty in this group,
    # then we can make all members traitors by choosing the traitor appropriately
    
    # Check if there's any universe that has both Rick and Morty
    # We need to check if any universe appears both as a negative and positive value
    
    # Let's reconstruct the set of universes that have both
    universe_with_rikg = set()
    universe_with_morty = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            universe_with_rikg.add(-member)
        else:
            universe_with_morty.add(member)
    
    # Find intersection - universes that have both Rick and Morty
    common_universes = universe_with_rikg & universe_with_morty
    
    # If there are no common universes, then this group can have all traitors
    if len(common_universes) == 0:
        found_possible = True
        break

if found_possible:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. There are `m` groups, and people can join groups multiple times
4. The world ends if there's ANY group where ALL members are traitors
5. We need to determine if there EXISTS ANY scenario (assignment of traitors) where the world ends

## Key Insight

The world ends if there's a group where all members are traitors. We need to check if there's any valid assignment of traitors such that for at least one group, every member in that group is a traitor.

For a group to have all traitors, we need to assign traitor status to all members of that group. Since in each universe exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor, we need to consider the constraints:

- For a universe, if Rick is in a group and we want him to be a traitor, then Morty from that universe must be loyal
- If Morty is in a group and we want him to be a traitor, then Rick from that universe must be loyal

## Solution Strategy

For each group, we want to check if it's possible for ALL members of that group to be traitors simultaneously.

For a group to have all traitors:
1. For each member in the group:
   - If it's Rick from universe `u`, then Rick must be traitor → Morty in universe `u` must be loyal
   - If it's Morty from universe `u`, then Morty must be traitor → Rick in universe `u` must be loyal

2. For any universe, we can have at most one traitor (either Rick or Morty). So if a universe appears in both a Rick and a Morty role in the same group, we have a conflict.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, the conflict only arises if we try to make both Rick and Morty of the same universe traitors simultaneously.

For a given group to be all traitors:
- For each universe `u` that appears:
  - If Rick from `u` is in the group, then Rick must be traitor → Morty from `u` must be loyal
  - If Morty from `u` is in the group, then Morty must be traitor → Rick from `u` must be loyal
- If both Rick and Morty from the same universe are in the group, then we need both to be traitors, which is impossible.

So for each group, we check if there's a conflict:
- If the same universe has both Rick and Morty in the group, then it's impossible for all members to be traitors

But wait - this is only for a specific group. We need to check if there exists ANY scenario where at least one group has all traitors.

Actually, for each group, we can ask: is it possible for ALL members of this group to be traitors?

For a group to be possible:
- For each universe `u`:
  - If both Rick and Morty of universe `u` appear in the group → impossible (would require both to be traitors)
  - If only Rick appears → possible (Rick is traitor, Morty is loyal)
  - If only Morty appears → possible (Morty is traitor, Rick is loyal)

If any group satisfies this condition (no universe has both Rick and Morty), then we can construct a scenario where all members of that group are traitors, so the answer is "YES".

## Algorithm

1. For each group:
   - Keep track of which universes have Rick and which have Morty in this group
   - If any universe has both Rick and Morty → this group cannot have all traitors
   - If no universe has both → this group CAN have all traitors (so answer is YES)

2. If NO group can have all traitors, then answer is "NO"

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

should_cancel = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:]
    
    # Track which universes have Rick and which have Morty in this group
    rick_universes = set()
    morty_universes = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Negative means Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            rick_universes.add(universe)
        else:
            # Positive means Morty from universe member
            universe = member
            morty_universes.add(universe)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty in this group
    # If so, we cannot have all traitors in this group
    has_conflict = False
    for universe in rick_universes:
        if universe in morty_universes:
            has_conflict = True
            break
    
    # If no conflict, then it's possible for all members of this group to be traitors
    if not has_conflict:
        should_cancel = True
        break

print("YES" if should_cancel else "NO")
```

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: n=4, m=2
- Group 1: k=1, member=[-3] → Rick from universe 3 only → no conflict → can have all traitors → answer YES

Example 2: n=5, m=2
- Group 1: k=5, members=[3, -2, 1, -1, 5]
  - Universes: 3(M), 2(R), 1(M), 1(R) → universe 1 has both Rick and Morty → conflict
- Group 2: k=3, members=[-5, 2, 5]
  - Universes: 5(R), 2(M), 5(M) → universe 5 has both Rick and Morty → conflict
- Both groups have conflicts → answer NO

Example 3: n=7, m=2
- Group 1: k=3, members=[-1, 6, 7]
  - Universes: 1(R), 6(M), 7(M) → no universe has both → possible → answer YES

This matches the expected outputs! The solution is correct.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Since the giant heads have appeared in the sky all humanity is in danger, so all Ricks and Mortys from all parallel universes are gathering in groups to find a solution to get rid of them. 

There are n parallel universes participating in this event (n Ricks and n Mortys). I. e. each of n universes has one Rick and one Morty. They're gathering in m groups. Each person can be in many groups and a group can contain an arbitrary number of members.

Ricks and Mortys have registered online in these groups. So, a person can have joined a group more than once (developer of this website hadn't considered this possibility). [Image] 

Summer from universe #1 knows that in each parallel universe (including hers) exactly one of Rick and Morty from that universe is a traitor and is loyal, but no one knows which one. She knows that we are doomed if there's a group such that every member in that group is a traitor (they will plan and destroy the world). 

Summer knows that if there's a possibility that world ends (there's a group where all members are traitors) she should immediately cancel this event. So she wants to know if she should cancel the event. You have to tell her yes if and only if there's at least one scenario (among all 2^{n} possible scenarios, 2 possible scenarios for who a traitor in each universe) such that in that scenario the world will end.


-----Input-----

The first line of input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^4) — number of universes and number of groups respectively.

The next m lines contain the information about the groups. i-th of them first contains an integer k (number of times someone joined i-th group, k > 0) followed by k integers v_{i}, 1, v_{i}, 2, ..., v_{i}, k. If v_{i}, j is negative, it means that Rick from universe number  - v_{i}, j has joined this group and otherwise it means that Morty from universe number v_{i}, j has joined it.

Sum of k for all groups does not exceed 10^4.


-----Output-----

In a single line print the answer to Summer's question. Print "YES" if she should cancel the event and "NO" otherwise.


-----Examples-----
Input
4 2
1 -3
4 -2 3 2 -3

Output
YES

Input
5 2
5 3 -2 1 -1 5
3 -5 2 5

Output
NO

Input
7 2
3 -1 6 7
7 -5 4 2 4 7 -3 4

Output
YES



-----Note-----

In the first sample testcase, 1st group only contains the Rick from universe number 3, so in case he's a traitor, then all members of this group are traitors and so Summer should cancel the event.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. There are `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. There are `m` groups, each containing members from various universes
4. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario where at least one group has ALL members as traitors
5. If such a scenario exists, we print "YES", otherwise "NO"

## Key Insight

For a group to have all traitors, every member in that group must be a traitor. Since in each universe exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor, for a group to have all traitors, we need to check if there's a way to assign traitors such that every member of some group is a traitor.

For any group to potentially have all traitors, we need to ensure that for each member in that group, we can choose the traitor from their universe.

## Analysis

For a group to have all traitors, we need to check if we can assign traitors to make every member in the group a traitor. 

For a member from universe `u`:
- If it's Rick from universe `u`, then Rick must be the traitor
- If it's Morty from universe `u`, then Morty must be the traitor

The constraint is that in each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty can be the traitor.

So for a group to have all traitors, we need to check if we can assign traitors such that:
- For each Rick member, we assign Rick as traitor
- For each Morty member, we assign Morty as traitor
- And in each universe, we don't have both Rick and Morty as traitors

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, we need to check if there exists ANY scenario where at least one group has all traitors.

For a specific group to have all traitors:
- For each member in the group, we need that person to be the traitor
- But in each universe, we can only have one traitor (either Rick or Morty)

So for a group to have all traitors, we need that in each universe that appears in the group, we have at most one person from that universe in the group.

Actually, let me think again. For a group to have all traitors:
- If a universe appears with both Rick and Morty in the group, then we would need both Rick and Morty to be traitors, which is impossible (only one can be traitor)

So a group can have all traitors if and only if for each universe, at most one of Rick or Morty appears in the group.

But we also need to consider that we're looking for if there exists ANY scenario where at least one group has all traitors.

So we need to check if there exists a group such that in that group, for every universe, at most one of the two members (Rick or Morty) appears.

Wait, that's not quite right either. For a group to have all traitors in a scenario:
- We need to assign traitors such that every member in the group is a traitor
- This is possible if and only if for each universe, at most one of Rick or Morty appears in the group

Because if both Rick and Morty from the same universe appear in the group, we would need both to be traitors, which violates the constraint that exactly one is a traitor.

Therefore, we can solve this by:
1. For each group, check if it contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe
2. If any group contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe, then it's impossible for all members of that group to be traitors
3. But if a group contains only one member from each universe (or only Ricks or only Mortys), then we can assign traitors to make all members traitors

Actually, let me be more precise:

For a group to be able to have all traitors in some scenario:
- For each universe, at most one of Rick or Morty appears in the group
- If a universe appears with both Rick and Morty, then it's impossible (we can't have both be traitors)

So the answer is "YES" if and only if there exists at least one group where no universe appears with both Rick and Morty.

## Algorithm

For each group:
- Create a set of universes that appear in this group
- For each universe in the group, check if both Rick and Morty appear
- If in any group, we find that both Rick and Morty from the same universe appear, skip this group
- If we find a group where no universe has both Rick and Morty, return "YES"

But wait - is that sufficient? What about the case where a universe has only Rick or only Morty appearing? That's fine.

Actually, the condition is: a group can have all traitors if and only if for each universe, at most one of its members (Rick or Morty) appears in the group.

So if we find any group that satisfies this condition, then there exists a scenario where all members of that group are traitors, so we should cancel the event.

Therefore, the answer is "YES" if any group has the property that for every universe, at most one of its members (Rick or Morty) appears.

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

should_cancel = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:]
    
    # Track which universes have both Rick and Morty
    universe_has_both = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Negative means Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            # Check if Morty from this universe also appears
            # We need to check if Morty from universe appears in this group
            # We'll go through all members to find if Morty exists
            pass
    
    # Better approach: use a set to track which universes have presence
    # and specifically check for conflicts
    
    universe_rick = set()
    universe_morty = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            universe_rick.add(-member)
        else:
            universe_morty.add(member)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty
    has_conflict = False
    for universe in universe_rick:
        if universe in universe_morty:
            has_conflict = True
            break
    
    # If no conflict, then we can have all traitors in this group
    if not has_conflict:
        should_cancel = True
        break

if should_cancel:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

- Group 1: [-3] → universe_rick = {3}, universe_morty = {} → no conflict → YES
- Group 2: [-2, 3, 2, -3] → rick: {2,3}, morty: {3,2} → conflict at universe 2 and 3 → skip

So answer is YES ✅

Example 2:
- Group 1: [3, -2, 1, -1, 5] → rick: {2,1,5}, morty: {3,1,5} → conflict at universe 1 and 5
- Group 2: [-5, 2, 5] → rick: {5}, morty: {2,5} → conflict at universe 5

So no group has no conflict → answer is NO ✅

Example 3:
- Group 1: [-1, 6, 7] → rick: {1}, morty: {6,7} → no conflict at any universe (1 not in morty, 6,7 not in rick) → YES

So answer is YES ✅

This matches all examples!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. We have `m` groups, where members can be repeated (same person joining multiple times)
4. The world ends if there exists ANY group where ALL members are traitors
5. We need to determine if there's ANY scenario (among 2^n possible scenarios) where the world ends

## Key Insight

The world ends if there's a group where all members are traitors. For each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor.

For a group to have all traitors, we need to check if we can assign traitors such that:
- For each universe mentioned in the group, the specific person mentioned is the traitor
- No universe has both Rick and Morty as traitors (which is impossible by problem statement)

## Critical Observation

For a group to potentially have all traitors, we need to check if there's any "conflict" or if the group's members can all be traitors simultaneously.

The key insight is: **A group can have all traitors if and only if for each universe in the group, we don't have both Rick AND Morty from that universe in the group.**

Wait, let me reconsider. Since in each universe exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor, for a group to have all traitors, we need to be able to assign a traitor to each universe such that all members in the group are traitors.

For a group to potentially have all traitors:
- For each universe, we need to make sure that at most one of Rick or Morty from that universe appears in the group
- If both Rick and Morty from the same universe appear in the same group, then we cannot have all members as traitors (since only one can be traitor)

This is the crucial insight!

## Algorithm

For each group:
1. Check if there exists any universe where both Rick and Morty appear in this group
2. If such a universe exists, then this group cannot have all traitors (because only one of Rick or Morty can be traitor)
3. If no such universe exists, then we can potentially have this group having all traitors (by making the member from each universe the traitor)

But we need to check if there's ANY scenario where the world ends. So if there exists at least one group where we don't have both Rick and Morty from the same universe, then we can assign traitors to make that group have all traitors.

Therefore, the answer is "YES" if and only if there exists at least one group where for every universe, we don't have both Rick and Morty from that universe.

Wait, let me think again. Actually, we need to check if there exists ANY group that can have all traitors.

For a group to be able to have all traitors:
- We need to ensure that for each universe, if both Rick and Morty are in the group, this is impossible
- If the group contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe, then it's impossible for all members to be traitors

So the algorithm:
1. For each group, check if it contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe
2. If any group does NOT contain both from the same universe, then YES (because we can make that group have all traitors)
3. If ALL groups contain at least one universe where both Rick and Morty appear, then NO

Actually, let me re-read the problem. We want to know if there's ANY scenario where the world ends (i.e., there's a group with all traitors).

So we need to check if there exists at least one group such that we can assign traitors to make all members in that group traitors.

For a group to be "possible", we need that for every universe in the group, at most one of Rick or Morty appears in the group.

Therefore, the answer is "YES" if there exists at least one group where no universe has both Rick and Morty appearing.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Check each group
any_possible = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:]
    
    # Track universes that have both Rick and Morty in this group
    has_both = False
    
    # For each member in the group
    universe_ricks = set()
    universe_mortys = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Negative means Rick from universe -member
            universe_ricks.add(-member)
        else:
            # Positive means Morty from universe member
            universe_mortys.add(member)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty
    for universe in universe_ricks:
        if universe in universe_mortys:
            has_both = True
            break
    
    # If no universe has both Rick and Morty, then this group can have all traitors
    if not has_both:
        any_possible = True
        break

if any_possible:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. There are `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. People can join groups multiple times
4. The world ends if there exists ANY group where ALL members are traitors
5. We need to determine if there exists at least one scenario (traitor assignment) where the world ends

## Key Insight

The world ends if there's a group where all members are traitors. For a group to have all traitors, we need to find a scenario where for every member in that group:
- If it's Rick from universe `i`, then Rick from universe `i` is a traitor
- If it's Morty from universe `i`, then Morty from universe `i` is a traitor

Since in each universe exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor, for each universe, we have two choices.

For a specific group to have all traitors, we need:
- For each member in the group, the traitor assignment must make that person a traitor
- This creates constraints on which person from each universe is the traitor

## Critical Observation

For a group to have all traitors, we need to satisfy the constraint that for each member in that group, that specific person is the traitor. 

For a group to be problematic (all members traitors), we need to find if there's any group that can simultaneously have all its members as traitors.

For a group with members from different universes, we need to check if we can assign traitors such that:
- All members in the group are traitors
- For each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor

The key insight is that a group can have all traitors if and only if for every universe that appears in the group:
- The group contains either Rick from that universe OR Morty from that universe (only one of them)
- But we need to check if there's a consistent assignment

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we have exactly one traitor per universe, for a group to have all members as traitors:
- For each universe that appears in the group, exactly one of Rick or Morty must be the traitor
- And that traitor must be one of the members in the group

So for a group, if it contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe, then we have a contradiction - we can't have both as traitors (only one can be a traitor).

If a group contains only one of Rick or Morty from a universe, then we can choose the traitor to be that one.

Therefore, a group can have all traitors if and only if for every universe mentioned in the group, the group contains exactly one of Rick or Morty from that universe (not both).

## Algorithm

1. For each group, check if it contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe
2. If any group contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe, then it's impossible for all members to be traitors in that group (since only one of them can be a traitor)
3. If a group contains only one type (either only Ricks or only Mortys) from each universe, then we can assign the traitor accordingly
4. We need to check if there exists ANY group that can have all traitors

Actually, let me reconsider: if a group contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe, then we cannot have both as traitors (since only one can be a traitor in that universe), so that group cannot have all traitors.

If a group contains only one of Rick or Morty from each universe, then we can assign the traitor to be that one, and it's possible for all members to be traitors.

Therefore, we just need to check if there exists any group such that for every universe mentioned in the group, the group contains exactly one of Rick or Morty from that universe.

## Step-by-step solution

1. Read input
2. For each group, build a set of universes mentioned
3. For each universe in the group, check if both Rick and Morty are present
4. If any group has both Rick and Morty from the same universe, skip it
5. If any group has only one of Rick or Morty from each universe, then it's possible to have all traitors in that group
6. If such a group exists, answer is "YES", otherwise "NO"

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

def has_both_rick_morty(group):
    """Check if the group contains both Rick and Morty from any universe"""
    universe_to_members = {}
    
    for member in group:
        if member < 0:
            universe = -member
            person = "rick"
        else:
            universe = member
            person = "morty"
        
        if universe not in universe_to_members:
            universe_to_members[universe] = []
        universe_to_members[universe].append(person)
    
    # Check if any universe has both rick and morty
    for universe, members in universe_to_members.items():
        if "rick" in members and "morty" in members:
            return True
    return False

# Check each group
should_cancel = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:k+1]
    
    # Check if this group has both Rick and Morty from any universe
    if not has_both_rick_morty(members):
        should_cancel = True
        break

if should_cancel:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Since the giant heads have appeared in the sky all humanity is in danger, so all Ricks and Mortys from all parallel universes are gathering in groups to find a solution to get rid of them. 

There are n parallel universes participating in this event (n Ricks and n Mortys). I. e. each of n universes has one Rick and one Morty. They're gathering in m groups. Each person can be in many groups and a group can contain an arbitrary number of members.

Ricks and Mortys have registered online in these groups. So, a person can have joined a group more than once (developer of this website hadn't considered this possibility). [Image] 

Summer from universe #1 knows that in each parallel universe (including hers) exactly one of Rick and Morty from that universe is a traitor and is loyal, but no one knows which one. She knows that we are doomed if there's a group such that every member in that group is a traitor (they will plan and destroy the world). 

Summer knows that if there's a possibility that world ends (there's a group where all members are traitors) she should immediately cancel this event. So she wants to know if she should cancel the event. You have to tell her yes if and only if there's at least one scenario (among all 2^{n} possible scenarios, 2 possible scenarios for who a traitor in each universe) such that in that scenario the world will end.


-----Input-----

The first line of input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^4) — number of universes and number of groups respectively.

The next m lines contain the information about the groups. i-th of them first contains an integer k (number of times someone joined i-th group, k > 0) followed by k integers v_{i}, 1, v_{i}, 2, ..., v_{i}, k. If v_{i}, j is negative, it means that Rick from universe number  - v_{i}, j has joined this group and otherwise it means that Morty from universe number v_{i}, j has joined it.

Sum of k for all groups does not exceed 10^4.


-----Output-----

In a single line print the answer to Summer's question. Print "YES" if she should cancel the event and "NO" otherwise.


-----Examples-----
Input
4 2
1 -3
4 -2 3 2 -3

Output
YES

Input
5 2
5 3 -2 1 -1 5
3 -5 2 5

Output
NO

Input
7 2
3 -1 6 7
7 -5 4 2 4 7 -3 4

Output
YES

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. There are `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. People can join groups multiple times
4. The world ends if there exists ANY group where ALL members are traitors
5. We need to determine if there's AT LEAST ONE scenario (assignment of traitors) where the world ends

## Key Insight

For the world to end, there must be some group where all members are traitors. 

In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor. So for any group to have all traitors:
- For each member in the group, that person must be the traitor in their universe
- We need to check if there's any group where we can assign traitors such that all members in that group are traitors

## Analysis

For a group to have all traitors, we need to check if we can assign traitors such that:
- Every member in the group is the traitor in their universe

However, in each universe, we can only choose one of Rick or Morty to be the traitor. So for a group to have all traitors, there must be no contradiction in the assignments.

The key insight is: **A group can have all traitors if and only if for every universe, there is exactly one person (Rick or Morty) in the group from that universe.**

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, in each universe, we can choose either Rick OR Morty to be the traitor. So for a group to have all traitors:
- For each universe, we must choose the specific person who appears in that group as the traitor
- But we can't have both Rick and Morty from the same universe in the group, because then we would have a contradiction (we can't make both traitors)

Actually, let me think more carefully:

For a group to have all traitors:
- For each universe, we must assign the traitor to be the person who appears in the group
- If a universe appears twice in the group (once as Rick and once as Morty), this is impossible because we can only choose one traitor

But the input says a person can join a group more than once. So we might have multiple entries for the same person.

The crucial point is: **For a group to have all traitors, in each universe, at most one of Rick or Morty can be present in the group.** If both are present, then we can't make both of them traitors (since only one can be a traitor per universe).

Wait, no - that's not right. The condition is: we need to assign traitors such that every member in the group is a traitor. 

For a universe to contribute to having all traitors in a group:
- If only Rick is present in the group from that universe, we can make Rick the traitor
- If only Morty is present in the group from that universe, we can make Morty the traitor  
- If both Rick and Morty are present in the group from the same universe, we cannot make both traitors (only one can be a traitor per universe)

Therefore, for a group to have all traitors, we need that for each universe, at most one of Rick or Morty appears in that group.

## Algorithm

For each group:
- Check if there's any universe that appears as both Rick and Morty in that group
- If such a universe exists, then this group CANNOT have all traitors (because we can't make both traitors)
- If no such universe exists, then we can assign traitors such that all members in this group are traitors (just make the person from each universe the traitor)

If there exists at least one group where no universe has both Rick and Morty, then the answer is "YES".

Wait, let me verify with the first example:
- Group 1: [-3] → Rick from universe 3
- Group 2: [-2, 3, 2, -3] → Rick from 2, Morty from 3, Morty from 2, Rick from 3

Group 1: Only Rick from universe 3 → no contradiction, so this group can have all traitors (if Rick from universe 3 is traitor)

Group 2: 
- Universe 2: Rick and Morty both appear → contradiction
- Universe 3: Rick and Morty both appear → contradiction

So only group 1 can have all traitors, so answer is "YES" - matches expected output.

Second example:
- Group 1: [3, -2, 1, -1, 5] → Morty from 3, Morty from 2, Morty from 1, Rick from 1, Morty from 5
- Universe 1: Morty and Rick both appear → contradiction
- Group 2: [-5, 2, 5] → Rick from 5, Morty from 2, Morty from 5

For group 2: Universe 5: Rick and Morty both appear → contradiction

So no group can have all traitors? But expected output is "NO".

Wait, I think I misunderstood. Let me re-read.

Actually, the condition is: we need to check if THERE EXISTS a scenario where the world ends (i.e., there exists a group where all members are traitors).

For each group, we can check if it's possible for all members to be traitors in some scenario. If any group has this property, answer is "YES".

For a group to have all traitors in some scenario:
- For each universe, at most one of Rick or Morty can be present in the group
- If both are present from the same universe, it's impossible

So if ANY group satisfies that condition (no universe has both Rick and Morty), then answer is "YES".

Let me check second example:
Group 1: [3, -2, 1, -1, 5]
- Universe 1: Morty (1) and Rick (-1) → both appear → impossible
- Universe 2: Morty (-2) → only Morty → ok  
- Universe 3: Morty (3) → only Morty → ok
- Universe 5: Morty (5) → only Morty → ok

Wait, universe 1 has both Rick (-1) and Morty (1) → contradiction, so group 1 cannot have all traitors.

Group 2: [-5, 2, 5]
- Universe 2: Morty (2) → only Morty → ok
- Universe 5: Rick (-5) and Morty (5) → both appear → contradiction

So both groups have contradictions, meaning no group can have all traitors → answer is "NO" → matches expected output.

Perfect! The algorithm is:

For each group:
1. Create a set to track which universes have both Rick and Morty
2. For each member in the group:
   - If negative, it's Rick from universe -value
   - If positive, it's Morty from universe value
3. For each universe, check if both Rick and Morty appear
4. If any universe has both, skip this group
5. If a group has no universe with both Rick and Morty, return "YES"

If no group satisfies the condition, return "NO".

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:]
    
    # Track which universes have both Rick and Morty
    universe_has_both = set()
    
    # Process each member
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
        else:
            # Morty from universe member
            universe = member
        
        # If this universe already appears in the group as the other type, we have a conflict
        # We need to track if both have appeared
        # This is tricky - we need to check if we've seen the other type before
        pass
    
    # Let's restructure: for each universe, keep track of whether we've seen Rick and whether we've seen Morty
    rick_seen = set()
    morty_seen = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            universe = -member
            rick_seen.add(universe)
        else:
            universe = member
            morty_seen.add(universe)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty
    has_both = False
    for universe in rick_seen:
        if universe in morty_seen:
            has_both = True
            break
    
    # If no universe has both Rick and Morty, then this group can have all traitors
    if not has_both:
        print("YES")
        exit()

# If we get here, no group can have all traitors
print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. There are `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. People join groups (can join multiple times, and a person can be in multiple groups)
4. The world ends if ANY group has ALL members as traitors
5. We need to determine if THERE EXISTS ANY scenario where the world ends

## Key Insight

The world ends if there's a group where all members are traitors. We need to check if this is possible in ANY scenario.

For a group to have all traitors, for each member in that group:
- If it's Rick from universe `u`, then Rick from universe `u` must be the traitor
- If it's Morty from universe `u`, then Morty from universe `u` must be the traitor

In each universe, only one of Rick or Morty can be the traitor. So for a group to have all traitors, we need consistent assignments across all members of that group.

For a group to be all traitors, the assignments must be consistent with the constraint that in each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor.

## Algorithm

For each group, we need to check if there's a way to assign traitors such that all members of this group are traitors.

For a group to work:
- Consider each member in the group
- For each universe, if we have both Rick and Morty from that universe in the group, then we have a contradiction - we can't have both as traitors
- If we have only one person from a universe in the group, that person must be the traitor
- If we have both Rick and Morty from the same universe, it's impossible for both to be traitors

So for a group to have all traitors:
1. For each universe, we should not have both Rick and Morty from that universe
2. The assignments must be consistent

Actually, let me reconsider: if we have both Rick and Morty from the same universe in a group, then they can't both be traitors (only one traitor per universe). So such a group cannot have all traitors.

But if we have only one person from each universe, then we can assign that person to be the traitor, and the other person in that universe would be loyal.

So the condition is: a group can have all traitors if and only if for every universe, we don't have both Rick and Morty from that universe in the group.

Wait, that's not quite right either. For a group to have all traitors, we need that for each member in the group:
- If it's Rick from universe u, then Rick from universe u is the traitor
- If it's Morty from universe u, then Morty from universe u is the traitor

But we can't have both Rick and Morty from the same universe being traitors. So if a group contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe, it's impossible for all members to be traitors.

Therefore, the world ends in some scenario if and only if there exists at least one group that does NOT contain both Rick and Morty from the same universe.

Actually, let me think again:

If a group contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe, then they can't both be traitors, so this group cannot have all traitors.

If a group contains only one person from each universe (either Rick or Morty), then we can make that person the traitor, and assign the other person to be loyal. This would make all members of the group traitors.

So the answer is "YES" if there exists at least one group such that for every universe, we don't have both Rick and Morty from that universe in the group.

In other words, if there's any group where no universe has both its Rick and Morty members appearing in that group, then we can have a scenario where all members of that group are traitors.

So the algorithm:
1. For each group, check if it contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe
2. If any group does NOT contain both from the same universe, then answer is "YES"
3. Otherwise, answer is "NO"

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: 
- Group 1: has Rick from universe 3 → only one person from universe 3 → no contradiction → all traitors possible
- Answer should be YES → matches

Example 2:
- Group 1: has 3 (Morty from 3), -2 (Rick from 2), 1 (Morty from 1), -1 (Rick from 1), 5 (Morty from 5)
  - Universe 1: has both Rick and Morty → contradiction → cannot have all traitors
- Group 2: has -5 (Rick from 5), 2 (Morty from 2), 5 (Morty from 5)
  - Universe 5: has both Rick and Morty → contradiction → cannot have all traitors

- Group 1: -1 (Rick from 1), 6 (Morty from 6), 7 (Morty from 7)

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

should_cancel = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:k+1]
    
    # Track which universes have both Rick and Morty
    universe_has_both = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            universe_has_both.add(universe)
        else:
            # Morty from universe member
            universe = member
            universe_has_both.add(universe)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty in this group
    # Actually, we need to check if we have both Rick and Morty from the same universe
    # We need to track which universes have Rick and which have Morty
    rick_universes = set()
    morty_universes = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            rick_universes.add(-member)
        else:
            morty_universes.add(member)
    
    # Check if there's any universe that has both Rick and Morty
    has_both = rick_universes & morty_universes
    
    # If no universe has both, then we can have all members as traitors
    if not has_both:
        should_cancel = True
        break

if should_cancel:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty in this group
    # Actually, we need to check if we have both Rick and Morty from the same universe
    # We need to track which universes have Rick and which have Morty
    rick_universes = set()
    morty_universes = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            rick_universes.add(-member)
        else:
            morty_universes.add(member)
    
    # Check if there's any universe that has both Rick and Morty
    has_both = rick_universes & morty_universes
    
    # If no universe has both, then we can have all members as traitors
    if not has_both:
        should_cancel = True
        break

if should_cancel:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Anadi has a set of dominoes. Every domino has two parts, and each part contains some dots. For every a and b such that 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, there is exactly one domino with a dots on one half and b dots on the other half. The set contains exactly 21 dominoes. Here is an exact illustration of his set:

<image>

Also, Anadi has an undirected graph without self-loops and multiple edges. He wants to choose some dominoes and place them on the edges of this graph. He can use at most one domino of each type. Each edge can fit at most one domino. It's not necessary to place a domino on each edge of the graph.

When placing a domino on an edge, he also chooses its direction. In other words, one half of any placed domino must be directed toward one of the endpoints of the edge and the other half must be directed toward the other endpoint. There's a catch: if there are multiple halves of dominoes directed toward the same vertex, each of these halves must contain the same number of dots.

How many dominoes at most can Anadi place on the edges of his graph?

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n ≤ 7, 0 ≤ m ≤ (n⋅(n-1))/(2)) — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph.

The next m lines contain two integers each. Integers in the i-th line are a_i and b_i (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n, a ≠ b) and denote that there is an edge which connects vertices a_i and b_i.

The graph might be disconnected. It's however guaranteed that the graph doesn't contain any self-loops, and that there is at most one edge between any pair of vertices.

Output

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 dominoes where each domino has two parts with dots (a,b) where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place as many dominoes as possible on edges
4. When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we assign direction: one half goes to u and one to v
5. **Key constraint**: For any vertex, all domino halves directed toward it must have the same number of dots

## Key Insight

The constraint means that if we have multiple dominoes directed toward the same vertex, they all must have the same dot value on that side. This creates dependencies between edges incident to the same vertex.

For each vertex, we can assign a "label" (the number of dots) to all incoming domino halves. The constraint requires that all incoming halves to a vertex have the same value.

## Solution Approach

This is essentially a constraint satisfaction problem. We need to:

1. For each edge, decide which domino to place (if any)
2. For each vertex, all incoming domino halves must have the same value

We can use dynamic programming with bitmasks since n ≤ 7.

However, a more practical approach:
- Try all possible ways to assign "dot values" to vertices
- For each vertex, we can assign a value from 1 to 6 (the possible dot values)
- For each edge (u,v), we can place a domino with values (a,b) where a is the value assigned to u and b to v, OR (b,a) - but since we have dominoes with a ≤ b, we need to ensure that the smaller value goes to the vertex that has the smaller dot value

Wait, let me reconsider. For each edge (u,v), when we place a domino:
- We assign a value x to vertex u
- We assign a value y to vertex v  
- We need to place a domino with values (min(x,y), max(x,y))

But we can only use each domino type once. So we need to track which domino types we've used.

This becomes a complex combinatorial problem. Let me think about a different approach.

## Alternative Insight

Since we have constraints on each vertex (all incoming halves have same value), we can think of it as:
- Assign a value v[i] to each vertex i
- For each edge (u,v), we place a domino with values (min(v[u],v[v]), max(v[u],v[v]))

The key constraint is that we can't use the same domino type more than once.

So the algorithm:
1. Try all possible assignments of values to vertices (each value from 1 to 6)
2. For each assignment, for each edge, determine which domino is used (min, max of the two vertex values)
3. Count how many distinct domino types are used
4. But we can only use each domino type once, so if the same domino type appears on multiple edges, we can only count one of them

Actually, I think I misunderstood. We have exactly one domino of each type. So if the same domino type appears on multiple edges in our assignment, we can only use it once. But since the domino type is determined by the values at the endpoints, if two edges have the same (min,max) pair, they both would want the same domino type.

So the maximum number of dominoes we can place is the number of edges that have unique domino types, or we can choose which edges to place dominoes on.

Wait, let me re-read: "It's not necessary to place a domino on each edge of the graph." So we can skip edges.

For a given vertex assignment, for each edge, we can potentially place a domino with value (min(v[u],v[v]), max(v[u],v[v])). But if multiple edges require the same domino type, we can only place one domino of that type.

So for a given assignment of values to vertices, the maximum number of dominoes we can place is the number of distinct (min,max) pairs across edges, where we only count each distinct domino type once.

But this isn't right either. We can choose which edges to place dominoes on. For each edge, if we place a domino, it will use the domino type (min(v[u],v[v]), max(v[u],v[v])). Since we have only one of each type, we can place at most one domino per domino type.

So for a given vertex assignment, we can place at most min(number of edges, number of distinct domino types).

Actually, no - we can place at most one domino of each domino type, so if there are k distinct domino types among the edges, we can place k dominoes.

So algorithm:
1. Try all possible assignments of values to the n vertices (each from 1 to 6)
2. For each assignment, compute the set of domino types used by edges
3. The number of dominoes we can place is the size of this set
4. Return the maximum over all assignments

But n ≤ 7, and each vertex has 6 possible values, so 6^7 ≈ 279,936 which is manageable.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: 4 vertices, cycle of 4 edges. 
- If we assign all vertices the same value, say 1, then all edges would use domino (1,1). So we can place only 1 domino (since we can't use (1,1) twice).
- But the answer is 4, so this must be wrong.

Wait, maybe I made a mistake. If we assign different values, can we get more?

Let me think again. For a cycle of 4: edges (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (4,1)

Suppose:
- v[1]=1, v[2]=2, v[3]=3, v[4]=4
Then edges use domino types: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (1,4)
All are distinct, so we can place 4 dominoes. That matches!

So the algorithm is:
- Try all possible value assignments to vertices (each from 1 to 6)
- For each edge (u,v), the domino used has value (min(v[u],v[v]), max(v[u],v[v]))
- Count the number of distinct domino types across all edges
- The answer is the maximum count over all assignments

But we don't have to use all edges - we can skip edges. However, since we want to maximize the number of dominoes, and each domino type can be used only once, for a given assignment we can place exactly one domino for each distinct domino type that appears on some edge.

So yes, for each assignment, we place one domino per distinct (min,max) pair.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    u, v = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # convert to 0-indexed

if m == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values to vertices (1 to 6)
    # Use recursion or nested loops
    from itertools import product
    
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # Create set of domino types used by edges
        domino_types = set()
        
        for u, v in edges:
            a, b = values[u], values[v]
            domino = (min(a, b), max(a, b))
            domino_types.add(domino)
        
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, len(domino_types))
    
    print(max_dominoes)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 dominoes (all pairs a,b where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6)
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge gets at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we assign direction: one half to u, one half to v
   - For any vertex, all domino halves pointing to it must have the same number of dots

## Key Insights

1. The constraint means that if multiple dominoes are directed toward the same vertex, they must all have the same dot value on that side.

2. For each vertex, if we decide that vertices connected to it have domino halves with certain values, those values must be consistent across all edges incident to that vertex.

3. This is essentially a constraint satisfaction problem where we need to assign values to vertices such that for each edge (u,v), the domino values on u and v are consistent.

4. For an edge (u,v), if we place domino (a,b), we can assign a to vertex u and b to vertex v, or b to vertex u and a to vertex v.

5. The constraint requires that for each vertex, all its incident edges must have the same value on that vertex.

If we assign different values, then the number of times a domino type is used is equal to the number of edges between vertices with those values.

This is equivalent to: find an assignment of values (1-6) to vertices, and then for each pair of values (a,b) with a≤b, the number of edges between vertices with values a and b is at most 1.

In other words, for each pair of values (a,b), we can place at most one domino of type (a,b), and we can only place it on one edge between vertices with values a and b.

So the algorithm is:

1. Try all possible assignments of values from 1 to 6 to the n vertices (n ≤ 7, so 6^n possibilities)
2. For each assignment, for each pair (a,b) with a ≤ b, count the number of edges between vertices with value a and value b
3. The number of dominoes we can place is the sum over all (a,b) of min(1, count of edges between a and b)
4. Take the maximum over all assignments

The maximum possible is when no two edges have the same (a,b) type.

In a complete graph with 7 vertices, if we can assign values so that no two edges have the same (a,b) type, then we could place 21 dominoes.

So the algorithm is correct.

Now I'll implement it.

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    edges = []
    if m > 0:
        for _ in range(m):
            u, v = map(int, input().split())
            edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # convert to 0-indexed
    
    if m == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values 1-6 to each vertex
    # n <= 7, so 6^n is at most 6^7 = 279936, which is acceptable
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, count how many edges we can place
        # We can place at most one domino of each type (a,b) with a<=b
        # For each edge between i and j, if values[i] and values[j] are a and b, then domino type is (min(a,b), max(a,b))
        
        # Count frequency of each domino type
        domino_count = {}
        for u, v in edges:
            a, b = values[u], values[v]
            domino_key = (min(a, b), max(a, b))
            domino_count[domino_key] = domino_count.get(domino_key, 0) + 1
        
        # For each domino type, we can use at most one domino
        # So the number of dominoes we can place is the number of unique domino types
        dominoes = len(domino_count)
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, dominoes)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()
```

from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    edges = []
    if m > 0:
        for _ in range(m):
            u, v = map(int, input().split())
            edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # convert to 0-indexed
    
    if m == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values 1-6 to each vertex
    # n <= 7, so 6^n is at most 6^7 = 279936, which is acceptable
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, count how many edges we can place
        # We can place at most one domino of each type (a,b) with a<=b
        # For each edge between i and j, if values[i] and values[j] are a and b, then domino type is (min(a,b), max(a,b))
        
        # Count frequency of each domino type
        domino_count = {}
        for u, v in edges:
            a, b = values[u], values[v]
            domino_key = (min(a, b), max(a, b))
            domino_count[domino_key] = domino_count.get(domino_key, 0) + 1
        
        # For each domino type, we can use at most one domino
        # So the number of dominoes we can place is the number of unique domino types
        dominoes = len(domino_count)
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, dominoes)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Anadi has a set of dominoes. Every domino has two parts, and each part contains some dots. For every a and b such that 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, there is exactly one domino with a dots on one half and b dots on the other half. The set contains exactly 21 dominoes. Here is an exact illustration of his set:

<image>

Also, Anadi has an undirected graph without self-loops and multiple edges. He wants to choose some dominoes and place them on the edges of this graph. He can use at most one domino of each type. Each edge can fit at most one domino. It's not necessary to place a domino on each edge of the graph.

When placing a domino on an edge, he also chooses its direction. In other words, one half of any placed domino must be directed toward one of the endpoints of the edge and the other half must be directed toward the other endpoint. There's a catch: if there are multiple halves of dominoes directed toward the same vertex, each of these halves must contain the same number of dots.

How many dominoes at most can Anadi place on the edges of his graph?

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n ≤ 7, 0 ≤ m ≤ (n⋅(n-1))/(2)) — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph.

The next m lines contain two integers each. Integers in the i-th line are a_i and b_i (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n, a ≠ b) and denote that there is an edge which connects vertices a_i and b_i.

The graph might be disconnected. It's however guaranteed that the graph doesn't contain any self-loops, and that there is at most one edge between any pair of vertices.

Output

Output one integer which denotes the maximum number of dominoes which Anadi can place on the edges of the graph.

Examples

Input


4 4
1 2
2 3
3 4
4 1


Output


4


Input


7 0


Output


0

3 1
1 3


Output


1

1. We have a set of 21 dominoes where each domino has two parts with dots (a, b) where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6

4. When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we choose direction: either (a→u, b→v) or (a→v, b→u)
5. **Constraint**: All domino halves directed toward the same vertex must have the same number of dots

1. The constraint means that for each vertex, all incoming domino halves must have the same value

   - Place domino (v,u) with v going to u and u going to v
   - Place domino (u,v) with u going to v and v going to u
4. But the constraint forces that all edges incident to u must have the same value at u, and all edges incident to v must have the same value at v

1. Try different values for each vertex (1 to 6)

   - a = v_u and b = v_v (domino goes a→u, b→v)

So for a fixed vertex assignment, the maximum number of dominoes we can place is equal to the number of edges we can assign without reusing domino types.

But this is still complex.

However, notice that the domino type for edge (u,v) depends only on v[u] and v[v]. 

For a fixed assignment of vertex values, each possible domino type (a,b) with a ≤ b appears as many times as there are edges (u,v) such that {v[u], v[v]} = {a,b}.

We can place a domino on such an edge only if we haven't used domino (a,b) elsewhere.

So the maximum number of dominoes we can place for this assignment is the number of edges whose domino types are distinct.

This is equivalent to the maximum matching in a different sense.

Actually, we can think of it as: for each domino type, we can use it at most once, and we can place it on any edge that requires that domino type.

For a given vertex assignment, the number of available dominoes is the number of edges where the domino type (min(v[u], v[v]), max(v[u], v[v])) is unique across all edges.

But we can choose which edges to place dominoes on - we don't have to place on all edges.

So for a fixed vertex assignment, we can place a domino on an edge if the domino type for that edge is not used by any other edge in our selection.

Therefore, the maximum number of dominoes for this assignment is the maximum number of edges we can select such that all the domino types are distinct.

This is equivalent to: we have a set of edges with associated domino types. We want to select the maximum number of edges such that no two selected edges have the same domino type.

This is just the number of edges with distinct domino types.

But since we can choose which edges to select, and the constraint is only on distinct domino types, we can select one edge per distinct domino type.

So for a given vertex assignment, the maximum number of dominoes we can place is equal to the number of distinct domino types among the edges.

For example, if all edges have the same domino type (1,3), then we can place only one domino.

If edges have different domino types, we might be able to place more.

But we want to maximize over all vertex assignments.

So the algorithm is:

1. Try all possible assignments of values to vertices (each value from 1 to 6)
2. For each assignment:
   - For each edge (u,v), compute the domino type (min(v[u], v[v]), max(v[u], v[v]))
   - Count the number of distinct domino types
3. Return the maximum over all assignments
