I think I have to go with the algorithm: try all assignments, for each assignment, count distinct domino types among edges, take maximum.

For the given examples, it should work.

Let me implement it.

Note: since n ≤ 7, 6^7 = 279,936, which is acceptable.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((a-1, b-1))  # convert to 0-indexed

# If no edges, answer is 0
if m == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values to vertices
    # Each vertex gets a value from 1 to 6
    from itertools import product
    
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, compute the domino type for each edge
        domino_types = set()
        for u, v in edges:
            a, b = values[u], values[v]
            # The domino type is (min, max)
            min_val = min(a, b)
            max_val = max(a, b)
            domino_types.add((min_val, max_val))
        
        # The number of distinct domino types is the number of dominoes we can place
        # (since each type can be used only once)
        num_dominoes = len(domino_types)
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, num_dominoes)
    
    print(max_dominoes)
```

Let's verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=4, m=4, edges: (0,1), (1,2), (2,3), (3,0)

Another possibility: maybe the domino type (a,b) with a=b is included, and we have to count (a,a) as well.

In the complete graph, if we have value a appearing at least twice, then (a,a) appears.

In our count above, we only counted (min(a,b), max(a,b)) for a≠b.

For a=b, when we have edge between two vertices with value a, we get domino (a,a).

So we need to include (a,a) for each value a that appears at least twice.

So for a value a that appears k times, we get edge type (a,a) for each pair of vertices with value a.

But since we only care about distinct types, if value a appears at least twice, we get one distinct type (a,a), regardless of how many pairs.

So the total distinct domino types = number of distinct unordered pairs (a,b) with a≤b, where a,b are values that appear.

This includes:
- All pairs (a,b) with a≠b: C(|S|,2) where S is the set of values
- All pairs (a,a) for a in S, if a appears at least twice

The number of (a,a) types is the number of values a that appear at least twice.

So total distinct domino types = C(|S|,2) + number of values that appear at least twice.

For example, if values are 1,2,3,4,5,6,6:
- S = {1,2,3,4,5,6}, |S|=6, C(6,2)=15
- Values that appear at least twice: {6}, so 1
- Total = 15 + 1 = 16

Yes! This matches the expected output of 16.

For values 1,2,3,4,5,6,6, we get:
- Pairs with distinct values: 15
- Pair (6,6): 1
- Total distinct types: 16

Perfect.

So the algorithm is:

For each assignment of values to vertices:
- Let S be the set of distinct values that appear
- Let k be the number of values in S that appear at least twice
- Total distinct domino types = C(|S|,2) + k

We need to compute this.

C(|S|,2) = |S|*(|S|-1)//2

k = number of values in S that have frequency >=2

We can compute this from the values array.

So let me modify the code:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((a-1, b-1))  # convert to 0-indexed

# If no edges, answer is 0
if m == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    from itertools import product
    
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, compute the distinct domino types
        # including (a,a) if a appears at least twice
        from collections import Counter
        value_count = Counter(values)
        
        # Get set of distinct values
        distinct_values = set(values)
        # Count how many values appear at least twice
        k = 0
        for val in distinct_values:
            if value_count[val] >= 2:
                k += 1
        
        # Number of distinct unordered pairs of distinct values
        s = len(distinct_values)
        distinct_pairs = s * (s - 1) // 2
        
        # Total distinct domino types = distinct_pairs + k
        num_dominoes = distinct_pairs + k
        
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, num_dominoes)
    
    print(max_dominoes)
```

This should work.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: values [1,2,3,4] for vertices 0,1,2,3
- distinct_values = {1,2,3,4}, s=4, distinct_pairs = 4*3//2 = 6
- values appear once, so k=0
- total = 6, but expected is 4.

So the distinct domino types are only those that appear in the edges, not all possible pairs.

I made a mistake. We only get a domino type if there is an edge between two vertices with those values.

So we need to compute the set of (min(a,b), max(a,b)) for each edge, not the universe of possible pairs.

In example 1, with values [1,2,3,4]:
- (0,1): (1,2)

So we must go back to the original plan: for each edge, compute the domino type (min, max), and count distinct types.

- For each pair of vertices with values, we get a domino type
- The distinct types are:

So the code is:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((a-1, b-1))

if m == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    from itertools import product
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        domino_types = set()
        for u, v in edges:
            a, b = values[u], values[v]
            min_val = min(a, b)
            max_val = max(a, b)
            domino_types.add((min_val, max_val))
        
        num_dominoes = len(domino_types)
        if num_dominoes > max_dominoes:
            max_dominoes = num_dominoes
    
    print(max_dominoes)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Anadi has a set of dominoes. Every domino has two parts, and each part contains some dots. For every a and b such that 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, there is exactly one domino with a dots on one half and b dots on the other half. The set contains exactly 21 dominoes. Here is an exact illustration of his set:

<image>

Also, Anadi has an undirected graph without self-loops and multiple edges. He wants to choose some dominoes and place them on the edges of this graph. He can use at most one domino of each type. Each edge can fit at most one domino. It's not necessary to place a domino on each edge of the graph.

When placing a domino on an edge, he also chooses its direction. In other words, one half of any placed domino must be directed toward one of the endpoints of the edge and the other half must be directed toward the other endpoint. There's a catch: if there are multiple halves of dominoes directed toward the same vertex, each of these halves must contain the same number of dots.

How many dominoes at most can Anadi place on the edges of his graph?

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n ≤ 7, 0 ≤ m ≤ (n⋅(n-1))/(2)) — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph.

The next m lines contain two integers each. Integers in the i-th line are a_i and b_i (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n, a ≠ b) and denote that there is an edge which connects vertices a_i and b_i.

The graph might be disconnected. It's however guaranteed that the graph doesn't contain any self-loops, and that there is at most one edge between any pair of vertices.

Output

Output one integer which denotes the maximum number of dominoes which Anadi can place on the edges of the graph.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 standard dominoes (all pairs a,b where 1≤a≤b≤6)
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge gets at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we choose direction: one half goes to u, one to v
   - For any vertex, all domino halves directed toward it must have the same number of dots

## Key Insight

The constraint means that if multiple dominoes are directed toward the same vertex, they must all have the same number of dots on that side. This creates a constraint on which dominoes can be placed on which edges.

## Approach

This is a constraint satisfaction problem. We can use backtracking/DFS to try all possible ways to assign dominoes to edges while satisfying the constraints.

However, since n ≤ 7 and we have at most 21 edges, we need an efficient approach.

The key insight is that for each vertex, we need to assign a "dot value" to all edges incident to it. If an edge connects vertices u and v, and we assign value x to u and value y to v, then we must place a domino with dots (x,y) or (y,x).

Since dominoes are symmetric (a,b is the same as b,a), we need to ensure that the pair (x,y) exists in our domino set.

## Algorithm

1. For each possible assignment of dot values to vertices, check if it's valid
2. For each edge, check if the domino (value_u, value_v) exists in our set
3. Count the maximum number of edges we can cover

But this would be too complex. Let me think of a simpler approach.

Since n ≤ 7, we could try all possible assignments of values 1-6 to each vertex (6^7 possibilities = 279,936), which is manageable.

For each assignment:
- For each edge (u,v), check if domino (value[u], value[v]) exists in the set
- Count how many edges satisfy this
- Keep track of the maximum

But we don't need to try all 6^7 assignments. Actually, we can use a more efficient approach:

We can represent the problem as: for each edge, we need to assign a domino type (a,b) where a,b ∈ {1,2,3,4,5,6} and a ≤ b.

For each vertex, all edges incident to it must have the same value on that side (the value must be consistent).

This means that if edge (u,v) is covered, then vertex u must have a value x, and vertex v must have a value y, and domino (x,y) must exist (x ≤ y or y ≤ x, but domino set has both (x,y) and (y,x) when x ≤ y).

Wait, actually in our domino set, we have exactly one domino for each pair (a,b) with 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6. So domino (a,b) exists iff a ≤ b.

For an edge (u,v), we can place domino (x,y) where x is the value at u, y is the value at v, and (x,y) is in the domino set (so either x ≤ y or y ≤ x, which is always true since we have all pairs).

The constraint is: all edges incident to vertex u must have the same value for the half going to u.

So the algorithm:

1. For each possible assignment of values 1-6 to each vertex (6^n possibilities):
   - Count how many edges can be covered (where domino (val[u], val[v]) exists)
   - Track maximum count
2. Return maximum count

Since n ≤ 7, 6^7 = 279,936, which is acceptable.

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # convert to 0-indexed
    
    # Generate all possible value assignments for vertices
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values 1-6 to each vertex
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # Count how many edges can be covered with this assignment
        count = 0
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = values[u], values[v]
            # Check if domino (val_u, val_v) exists
            # Domino exists if either val_u <= val_v or we can use the symmetric one
            # Actually, since our set has all pairs (a,b) with 1<=a<=b<=6, 
            # the domino (a,b) exists iff min(a,b) <= max(a,b) which is always true
            # But we need to check if the pair (min(val_u,val_v), max(val_u,val_v)) 
            # falls within 1 to 6, which it always does since values are 1-6
            count += 1
        
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, count)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()
```

But for a fixed assignment, the domino type for edge (u,v) is (min(val[u], val[v]), max(val[u], val[v])).

We can place a domino on edge (u,v) if and only if that domino type is valid (it is, since values are 1-6) and we haven't used it on any other edge.

So for a given assignment, the maximum number of edges we can cover is the number of distinct domino types that appear in edges.

Wait, no - if two edges have the same domino type, we can only use one of them.

So for a given assignment, we can cover at most one edge per domino type.

Therefore, for a fixed assignment, the maximum number of edges we can cover is the number of distinct domino types that appear in the edges in that assignment.

And we want to maximize this over all assignments.

For example 4: n=7, m=21, answer is 16.

So we need to find an assignment of values 1-6 to vertices that produces 16 distinct domino types.

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # convert to 0-indexed
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values 1-6 to each vertex
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, compute the required domino types for each edge
        domino_types = set()
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = values[u], values[v]
            # The domino type is (min, max) - we store it in sorted order
            domino = (min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v))
            domino_types.add(domino)
        
        # The number of dominoes we can place is the number of distinct domino types
        count = len(domino_types)
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, count)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Anadi has a set of dominoes. Every domino has two parts, and each part contains some dots. For every a and b such that 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, there is exactly one domino with a dots on one half and b dots on the other half. The set contains exactly 21 dominoes. Here is an exact illustration of his set:

<image>

Also, Anadi has an undirected graph without self-loops and multiple edges. He wants to choose some dominoes and place them on the edges of this graph. He can use at most one domino of each type. Each edge can fit at most one domino. It's not necessary to place a domino on each edge of the graph.

When placing a domino on an edge, he also chooses its direction. In other words, one half of any placed domino must be directed toward one of the endpoints of the edge and the other half must be directed toward the other endpoint. There's a catch: if there are multiple halves of dominoes directed toward the same vertex, each of these halves must contain the same number of dots.

How many dominoes at most can Anadi place on the edges of his graph?

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n ≤ 7, 0 ≤ m ≤ (n⋅(n-1))/(2)) — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph.

The next m lines contain two integers each. Integers in the i-th line are a_i and b_i (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n, a ≠ b) and denote that there is an edge which connects vertices a_i and b_i.

The graph might be disconnected. It's however guaranteed that the graph doesn't contain any self-loops, and that there is at most one edge between any pair of vertices.

Output

Output one integer which denotes the maximum number of dominoes which Anadi can place on the edges of the graph.

Examples

Input


4 4
1 2
2 3
3 4
4 1


Output


4


Input


7 0

0

1

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 dominoes where each domino has two halves with dots (a,b) where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge gets at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we orient it so one half goes to u and one to v
   - For each vertex, all domino halves directed toward it must have the same number of dots

## Key Insights

1. For each vertex, all incoming domino halves must have the same dot value
2. This creates constraints: if vertex v has degree d and receives domino halves with value x, then all edges incident to v must have one half directed to v with value x
3. For an edge (u,v), if we place a domino with values (a,b), then:
   - Either a goes to u and b goes to v (meaning u gets a, v gets b)
   - Or b goes to u and a goes to v (meaning u gets b, v gets a)
4. For vertex v, all its incident edges must have the same value for the half directed to v

## Approach

Since n ≤ 7, we can use a state-space search with bitmasks or try all possible assignments.

The key insight is that we can assign a dot value to each vertex (from 1 to 6) and then check if we can place dominoes on edges satisfying these constraints.

For each edge (u,v), if we assign value x to vertex u and value y to vertex v, then we need a domino with values (x,y) or (y,x). Since every domino (a,b) with a≤b exists, we only need that min(x,y) ≤ max(x,y) and we have the domino.

So the problem reduces to: assign values 1-6 to each vertex such that for each edge (u,v), the domino (min(val[u],val[v]), max(val[u],val[v])) exists (which it always does), and we maximize the number of edges that get dominoes.

But we don't need to place dominoes on all edges - we just need to count how many edges we can place dominoes on with valid assignments.

For a given assignment of values to vertices, for each edge (u,v) we can place a domino if we choose orientation such that the values match the assigned values for that vertex.

For edge (u,v), we can place a domino if we assign value x to u and y to v, and we can always place a domino (x,y) with x≤y or (y,x) with y≤x.

Since dominoes exist for all pairs (a,b) with 1≤a≤b≤6, we can always place a domino on any edge with any vertex assignments.

But the constraint is that for each vertex, all incoming domino halves must have the same value.

But since we have all dominoes from (1,1) to (6,6), we always have the domino (min(x,y), max(x,y)).

- Example 1: n=4, m=4, output=4 ✓

But why can't we place dominoes on all 21 edges in the complete graph?

We have exactly one domino of each type (a,b) where 1≤a≤b≤6. But we don't have to use it - we can place at most one domino per type in the graph.

For a given edge (u,v) with values assigned to u and v, we would need to use the domino (min(x,y), max(x,y)). 

Since we have only one domino of each type, we can use this domino on only one edge.

Therefore, if multiple edges require the same domino type (same (a,b) pair), we can only place one domino on such edges.

So the problem becomes:
- Assign values 1-6 to each vertex v (1 to n)
- For each edge (u,v), we need a domino of type (min(val[u],val[v]), max(val[u],val[v]))
- Since we have only one domino of each type, we can place at most one domino for each domino type
- We want to maximize the total number of edges that get dominoes

This is equivalent to: assign values to vertices, and for each edge, we get a domino type. We sum up the number of distinct domino types that are used, since each domino type can only be used once.

Wait no - we can use the same domino type on at most one edge. So if two edges both require domino type (2,3), we can only place one domino (either on first or second edge).

Therefore, for a given value assignment, the number of dominoes we can place equals the number of distinct domino types that are required by the edges.

So the algorithm:
1. Try all possible assignments of values (1-6) to each of the n vertices
2. For each assignment, determine which domino types are used by the edges
3. The number of dominoes we can place is the count of distinct domino types used
4. Take the maximum over all assignments

Since n ≤ 7 and each vertex has at most 6 values, total assignments are 6^n ≤ 6^7 = 279,936, which is computationally feasible.

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        # Convert to 0-indexed
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))
    
    if m == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible value assignments for vertices (each vertex gets a value from 1 to 6)
    for assignment in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, find the set of domino types used
        used_types = set()
        
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = assignment[u], assignment[v]
            # The domino type is (min, max)
            domino_type = (min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v))
            used_types.add(domino_type)
        
        # Count distinct domino types
        num_dominoes = len(used_types)
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, num_dominoes)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()
```

from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        # Convert to 0-indexed
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))
    
    if m == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible value assignments for vertices (each vertex gets a value from 1 to 6)
    for assignment in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, find the set of domino types used
        used_types = set()
        
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = assignment[u], assignment[v]
            # The domino type is (min, max)
            domino_type = (min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v))
            used_types.add(domino_type)
        
        # Count distinct domino types
        num_dominoes = len(used_types)
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, num_dominoes)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 dominoes where each domino has two parts with dots (a,b) where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge gets at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we choose a direction (u→v or v→u)
   - For each vertex, all domino halves pointing toward it must have the same number of dots
4. We want to maximize the number of dominoes placed

## Key Insight

The constraint that all domino halves pointing toward the same vertex must have the same number of dots means that:
- For each vertex, all incoming dominoes must have the same value on the side facing that vertex
- This creates a constraint on which dominoes can be placed on which edges

## Approach

Since n ≤ 7, we can use dynamic programming with bit manipulation or try all possible assignments.

However, a more straightforward approach is to realize that we need to assign a "value" to each vertex (the number of dots on the side facing that vertex), and then check which dominoes are valid for each edge.

For each edge (u,v), if we assign values v[u] and v[v] to vertices u and v, then:
- We can place a domino (a,b) on edge (u,v) only if either:
  - a = v[u] and b = v[v], or
  - a = v[v] and b = v[u] (but this means the domino is oriented the other way)

But since the domino (a,b) exists only when a ≤ b, and we're placing it with orientation, we actually need to consider that for edge (u,v) we can place domino (min(v[u],v[v]), max(v[u],v[v])).

Wait, that's not quite right. The domino (a,b) can be placed with either direction, so for edge (u,v), if we assign values v[u] and v[v] to vertices u and v, then we can place domino (min(v[u],v[v]), max(v[u],v[v])) on that edge.

So the constraint is: for each edge (u,v), we can place a domino if and only if the value (min(v[u],v[v]), max(v[u],v[v])) exists in our domino set (which it does for all pairs with 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6).

Actually, every such pair exists in the domino set. So the only constraint is that we can't place dominoes on edges that would violate the "same value" requirement for each vertex.

The key insight is that we can try all possible assignments of values (from 1 to 6) to each vertex, and for each assignment, count how many edges can have a domino placed on them.

For each vertex, we assign a value 1-6. For each edge (u,v), if we assign values val[u] and val[v] to u and v, then we can place a domino on it (specifically the domino (min(val[u],val[v]), max(val[u],val[v]))).

Since the domino set contains all such pairs (a,b) where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, we can always place a domino on an edge as long as we assign values to vertices.

The only constraint is that we can only use each domino type once. But since we're placing at most one domino per edge, and each domino is used at most once, we just need to count how many edges we can cover with the current vertex assignments.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

1. Try all possible assignments of values (1-6) to each of the n vertices
2. For each assignment, count how many edges can have a domino placed (which is all edges, since we can always place a domino)
3. But we need to ensure we don't use the same domino type twice

Wait, no - each domino type is unique, and we have exactly one of each type. So if two edges have the same (min(val[u],val[v]), max(val[u],val[v])) pair, we can't place dominoes on both.

So for each assignment, we need to:
- For each edge (u,v), compute the domino type needed: (min(val[u],val[v]), max(val[u],val[v]))
- Count how many distinct domino types we have, and the number of edges that can be satisfied is the number of edges that get a "valid" domino (which is all edges, but we can only use each domino once)

Actually, since each edge needs a domino type, and we can only use each domino type once, the maximum number of dominoes we can place with a given assignment is the number of edges, but constrained by the fact that if multiple edges require the same domino type, we can only place one domino of that type.

Therefore, for a given vertex assignment, the number of dominoes we can place is equal to the number of distinct domino types needed across all edges.

So the algorithm:
1. Try all possible assignments of values (1-6) to each vertex (6^n possibilities, n ≤ 7, so 6^7 = 279936, which is acceptable)
2. For each assignment, for each edge, compute the required domino type (min(val[u],val[v]), max(val[u],val[v]))
3. Count the number of distinct domino types across all edges
4. Take the maximum over all assignments

But we need to consider that we might not place a domino on every edge - we can choose which edges to place dominoes on. However, since placing a domino doesn't reduce the "validity" of the vertex constraints, and we're just trying to maximize the count, we should place dominoes on edges as long as they don't violate the domino type uniqueness.

Actually, for a given vertex assignment, we can place a domino on an edge if the domino type (min(val[u],val[v]), max(val[u],val[v])) is available (which it always is, since all such pairs exist in the domino set). But we can only use each domino type once.

So we can place at most min(m, number of distinct domino types) dominoes? No, that's not right either.

We can place at most one domino per domino type. So if we have k distinct domino types across all edges, we can place at most k dominoes.

But we might not want to place dominoes on all edges - we might choose a subset of edges that have distinct domino types.

Since we want to maximize the number, for a given assignment, we should place dominoes on edges that give us distinct domino types. The maximum we can achieve is the number of distinct domino types across all edges.

Therefore, the answer is: max over all vertex assignments of (number of distinct domino types among edges)

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=4, cycle of 4 vertices. If all vertices get value 3, then every edge needs domino (3,3). So only 1 distinct domino type, so we get 1 domino.

But the answer is 4. So there must be a better assignment.

If we assign values: vertex 1:3, vertex 2:3, vertex 3:3, vertex 4:3 - all edges get domino (3,3), so only 1 domino.

If we assign: vertex 1:1, vertex 2:2, vertex 3:3, vertex 4:4
Edge (1,2): (1,2)
Edge (2,3): (2,3) 
Edge (3,4): (3,4)
Edge (4,1): (1,4)

All these are distinct domino types: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (1,4). So 4 distinct domino types, which gives 4.

That matches the expected output.

Example 2: n=7, m=0. No edges, so answer is 0.

Example 3: n=3, one edge (1,3)
We can assign values to vertices 1,2,3. For edge (1,3), we get domino (min(v[1],v[3]), max(v[1],v[3])). Since we want to maximize the count, we just need one distinct type, so we get 1.

Example 4: n=7, complete graph. Answer is 16. This suggests our approach works.

So the algorithm is:

```python
from itertools import product

n, m = map(int, input().split())
edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    u, v = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # convert to 0-indexed

max_dominoes = 0

# Try all possible assignments of values (1-6) to each vertex
for assignment in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
    # For this assignment, compute domino types for each edge
    domino_types = set()
    for u, v in edges:
        val_u, val_v = assignment[u], assignment[v]
        a, b = min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v)
        domino_types.add((a, b))
    
    # The number of distinct domino types is the maximum number of dominoes we can place
    max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, len(domino_types))

print(max_dominoes)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 dominoes where each domino has two parts with dots ranging from 1 to 6, and for every a ≤ b, there's exactly one domino with a dots and b dots.

2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges.

3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge can have at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we choose a direction: either u gets value a and v gets value b, or u gets value b and v gets value a
   - For any vertex, if multiple domino halves are directed toward it, they must all have the same number of dots

4. Goal: Maximize the number of dominoes placed.

## Key Insights

The constraint that "all halves directed toward the same vertex must have the same number of dots" is crucial. This means that for each vertex, we assign a "dot value" (from 1 to 6), and all dominoes pointing to that vertex must have that specific value.

For any edge (u,v), if we place a domino with values (a,b), then:
- If we assign value x to vertex u and value y to vertex v, then the domino must have (x,y) or (y,x)

This means that for any edge (u,v), the values assigned to u and v must be one of the domino pairs in our set.

## Solution Approach

Since n ≤ 7, we can use a brute force approach with bit manipulation or backtracking to try all possible assignments of dot values to vertices.

For each vertex, we try all possible dot values from 1 to 6. This gives us 6^n possible assignments.

For each assignment, we check how many edges can have dominoes placed on them:
- For each edge (u,v), check if the pair (value[u], value[v]) or (value[v], value[u]) exists in our domino set
- Since dominoes exist for all pairs where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, we just need to check if the sorted pair (min(a,b), max(a,b)) is valid

For each edge (u,v) with values val_u and val_v:
- The domino (min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v)) exists in the domino set
- So we can place a domino on this edge

Therefore, the maximum number of dominoes is the maximum, over all valid vertex assignments, of the number of edges that satisfy the condition.

## Implementation

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((a, b))
    
    # If no edges, answer is 0
    if m == 0:
        return 0
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible dot value assignments for vertices 1 to n
    # Each vertex can have value from 1 to 6
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # Count how many edges can have dominoes placed
        dominoes_count = 0
        for u, v in edges:
            # Convert to 0-indexed for easier processing
            val_u = values[u - 1]
            val_v = values[v - 1]
            
            # For edge (u,v), we can place a domino if the pair (val_u, val_v) is valid
            # Since dominoes exist for all pairs (a,b) where 1 <= a <= b <= 6
            # We just need to check if the sorted pair exists
            a, b = min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v)
            
            # Since all pairs from (1,1) to (6,6) with 1 <= a <= b <= 6 exist
            # This is always valid for any values from 1 to 6
            # So we can always place a domino on this edge
            dominoes_count += 1
        
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, dominoes_count)
    
    return max_dominoes

print(solve())
```

```python
from itertools import product

n, m = map(int, input().split())

edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((a, b))

if m == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible dot value assignments for vertices 1 to n
    # Each vertex can have value from 1 to 6
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # Count how many edges can have dominoes placed
        dominoes_count = 0
        for u, v in edges:
            # For edge (u,v), we can place a domino if the pair of values exists
            # Since all pairs (a,b) with 1<=a<=b<=6 exist, we can always place a domino
            # as long as the values are between 1 and 6, which they are
            dominoes_count += 1
        
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, dominoes_count)
    
    print(max_dominoes)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 dominoes where each domino has two parts with dots (a, b) where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge gets at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge between vertices u and v, we choose a direction
   - For each vertex, all domino halves directed toward it must have the same number of dots
4. We want to maximize the number of dominoes placed

## Key Insights

1. For each vertex, all domino halves pointing to it must have the same number of dots. This means if vertex v has several domino halves pointing to it, they must all have the same dot count.

2. When we place a domino on edge (u,v) with direction from u to v, we assign dots a to vertex u and dots b to vertex v (or vice versa).

3. This creates constraints: if two edges share a vertex, the dominoes at that vertex must all have the same dot count.

4. This is essentially a constraint satisfaction problem where we need to assign dot values to vertices such that the constraints are satisfied.

- x = value[u] and y = value[v], or

So we need to select a subset of edges and assign a domino type to each edge such that:
1. For each vertex, all dominoes incident to it have the same number of dots on the half directed toward that vertex.
2. No domino type is used more than once.

This is a more complex constraint.

For a vertex v, if we have edges (v,u1), (v,u2), ..., (v,uk), then the value assigned to v (the dot count on the half directed to v) must be the same for all of these edges.

This means that for each edge (v,u), if we assign value x to v and value y to u, then the domino used must be either (x,y) or (y,x). Since we have all dominoes with 1≤a≤b≤6, we have both (min(x,y), max(x,y)) types.

But we can only use each domino type once.

So the problem is: assign to each vertex a value from 1 to 6 (or no value if no edge incident to it), and for each edge (u,v), we assign a domino type (a,b) where a and b are the values assigned to u and v respectively (with appropriate orientation).

The constraint is that no domino type appears more than once across all edges.

We want to maximize the number of edges.

Since n ≤ 7, we can try all possible assignments of values to vertices (each vertex can have value 0 to 6, where 0 means no domino is incident to it).

For each assignment, we can determine which domino type would be used for each edge, and count how many distinct domino types we use, and maximize the number of edges we can cover.

Actually, for each edge, we have two possible domino types: (min(val[u], val[v]), max(val[u], val[v])) and (max(val[u], val[v]), min(val[u], val[v])), but since we only have one domino type for a≤b, it's just the one with a≤b.

So for edge (u,v), the domino type used is (min(val[u], val[v]), max(val[u], val[v])).

The constraint is that no two edges can have the same domino type.

So for a given vertex assignment, the number of edges we can cover is the number of edges where the domino type (min(val[u], val[v]), max(val[u], val[v])) is unique.

We need to find the assignment that maximizes this count.

Since n ≤ 7 and values are from 0 to 6, there are at most 7 vertices, so 7^7 ≈ 823,543 possible assignments, which is manageable.

But we also have m ≤ 21, so we can solve this with brute force.

The steps:
1. Read input
2. Try all possible value assignments to vertices (value[i] from 0 to 6 for each vertex i)
3. For each assignment, for each edge (u,v), compute the domino type (min(val[u], val[v]), max(val[u], val[v]))
4. Count how many distinct domino types we have, and keep only those that are within 1≤a≤b≤6 (which they will be if val[u] and val[v] are from 0 to 6, since if both are 0, we get (0,0) which is invalid)
5. For edges where the domino type would be (0,0) or both values are 0, we skip (since no domino has 0 dots)
6. For each edge, if the domino type is valid and unique, we can place a domino
7. Actually, we can place a domino on edge (u,v) only if the domino type is valid (both values ≥1), and we haven't used that domino type on another edge
8. So we need to count the number of edges that can be assigned a unique valid domino type

For a fixed vertex assignment, we can place a domino on edge (u,v) if and only if val[u] ≥ 1 and val[v] ≥ 1, and the domino type (min(val[u], val[v]), max(val[u], val[v])) has not been used on any other edge.

We want to know the maximum number of such edges.

We can do this greedily: for each edge, if the domino type is valid and not used, we can use it.

So the algorithm for a given vertex assignment:
- Initialize a set of used domino types
- For each edge (u,v):
  - If val[u] ≥ 1 and val[v] ≥ 1:
    - domino_type = (min(val[u], val[v]), max(val[u], val[v]))
    - If domino_type not in used:
      - add it to used
      - count this edge
- Return count

Then we maximize over all vertex assignments.

Since 7^7 ≈ 823,543 is reasonable, we can implement this.

Let me verify with examples:
- Example 1: n=4, m=4, cycle of 4 vertices. We can likely achieve 4.
- Example 2: m=0, answer is 0.
- Example 3: n=3, m=1, answer is 1.
- Example 4: n=7, m=21, answer is 16.

I think this approach should work.

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        # Convert to 0-indexed
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))
    
    if m == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values to vertices
    # Each vertex gets a value from 0 to 6 (0 means no dot value assigned)
    for values in product(range(7), repeat=n):
        used_dominoes = set()
        count = 0
        
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = values[u], values[v]
            # Check if both values are at least 1 (valid domino)
            if val_u >= 1 and val_v >= 1:
                # Create domino type with a <= b
                a, b = min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v)
                domino = (a, b)
                if domino not in used_dominoes:
                    used_dominoes.add(domino)
                    count += 1
        
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, count)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()
```

from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        # Convert to 0-indexed
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))
    
    if m == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values to vertices
    # Each vertex gets a value from 0 to 6 (0 means no dot value assigned)
    for values in product(range(7), repeat=n):
        used_dominoes = set()
        count = 0
        
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = values[u], values[v]
            # Check if both values are at least 1 (valid domino)
            if val_u >= 1 and val_v >= 1:
                # Create domino type with a <= b
                a, b = min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v)
                domino = (a, b)
                if domino not in used_dominoes:
                    used_dominoes.add(domino)
                    count += 1
        
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, count)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

For a string S consisting of the uppercase English letters `P` and `D`, let the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S be the total number of occurrences of `D` and `PD` in S as contiguous substrings. For example, if S = `PPDDP`, it contains two occurrences of `D` and one occurrence of `PD` as contiguous substrings, so the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S is 3.

We have a string T consisting of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`, find one with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient.

Constraints

* 1 \leq |T| \leq 2 \times 10^5
* T consists of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


T


Output

Print one string with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`. If there are multiple such strings, you may print any of them.

Examples

Input

PD?D??P


Output

PDPDPDP


Input

P?P?


Output

PDPD

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string T with characters 'P', 'D', and '?'
2. We need to replace each '?' with either 'P' or 'D' to maximize the "doctoral and postdoctoral quotient"
3. The quotient is the total count of:
   - Occurrences of 'D' as a single character
   - Occurrences of 'PD' as a contiguous substring

## Analysis

Let me think about when each contribution is created:
- A 'D' character contributes 1 to the quotient
- A 'PD' substring contributes 1 to the quotient

So for each position i:
- If T[i] is 'D', it contributes 1 (for the 'D' itself)
- If T[i] is 'P' and T[i+1] is 'D', it contributes 1 (for the 'PD' substring)

## Strategy

I need to decide whether to put 'P' or 'D' in each '?' position to maximize the total count.

Let me consider the contribution of each position:
- Position i: if we put 'D', we get 1 for the single 'D'
- Position i: if we put 'P', and the next character is 'D', we get 1 for 'PD'

However, the choice at position i affects both:
- The single 'D' contribution at i
- The 'PD' contribution when i is followed by 'D'
- The 'PD' contribution when i-1 is 'P' and T[i] is 'D'

Wait, let me think more systematically.

For any position i:
- If T[i] = 'D': contributes 1 (single D)
- If T[i] = 'P' and T[i+1] = 'D': contributes 1 (PD substring)

So the contribution depends on both the current character and the next character.

## Greedy Approach

Let me think about each '?' position independently. For each '?', I need to decide if putting 'P' or 'D' gives more total contribution.

Consider a '?' at position i:
- If I put 'D':
  - Get 1 for the single 'D' at position i
  - If T[i+1] is 'P', then this D won't form PD with it
  - If T[i+1] is 'D', then we have 'DD' - no PD formed
  - If T[i+1] is '?', then we get PD only if we put 'P' at i+1
- If I put 'P':
  - Get 0 for single D (since it's P)
  - Get 1 for PD if T[i+1] is 'D' (and T[i+1] is not '?')
  - If T[i+1] is '?', we might get PD if we later put 'D' there

Actually, this is more complex because the decision at position i affects what we get at position i+1.

Let me think about the contribution of each possible pair:
- For each adjacent pair (i, i+1), we can get a PD if position i is 'P' and position i+1 is 'D'

So I should try to maximize the number of 'PD' pairs.

For each '?' position, I need to consider:
- Current character vs next character

A key insight: putting 'P' at a position creates potential for PD with the next character, while putting 'D' at a position contributes 1 directly but doesn't create a PD.

So I should consider the net benefit.

For position i with '?':
- If I put 'D': I get 1 for the D, but I don't get PD (since P+D = PD, not D+P)
- If I put 'P': I get 0 for single D, but I get PD if T[i+1] is 'D'

Wait, that's not quite right. If I put 'P' at i, I get PD with position i+1 only if position i+1 is 'D'. 

So for the decision at position i:
- Putting 'D': gives 1 point (single D)
- Putting 'P': gives 1 point only if T[i+1] is 'D' (either fixed or we choose it to be 'D')

But if T[i+1] is '?', then putting 'P' at i means I get 1 point only if I later put 'D' at i+1.

This suggests a dynamic programming approach, but maybe there's a simpler greedy solution.

## Simple Greedy Strategy

Looking at the pattern: PD patterns are beneficial. So I want to create as many PD pairs as possible.

The key insight: when I have a '?' followed by a '?' or followed by 'D', I should consider putting 'P' when possible to create PD.

But also, putting 'D' when I need a D character gives me a direct count.

Wait, let me reconsider: each 'D' contributes 1, each 'PD' contributes 1. So I want to maximize both.

Actually, let me try a greedy approach from left to right:
- For each '?' at position i:
  - If T[i+1] is 'D' or T[i+1] is '?', putting 'P' might be better because it creates a potential PD
  - But if T[i+1] is fixed as 'P', then putting 'D' gives me 1 point for the D, while putting 'P' gives 0

So the decision should be:
- If T[i+1] is 'P': put 'D' (get 1 point)
- If T[i+1] is 'D' or '?': put 'P' (get potential for PD)

But if I put 'P' at i when T[i+1] is '?', I get 1 point only if I later put 'D' at i+1.

Similarly, if I put 'D' at i when T[i+1] is '?', I get 1 point now, but if I later put 'P' at i+1, I get no PD.

This is complex. Let me think about the net gain.

Actually, I can use a greedy strategy from right to left or left to right to determine the optimal choices.

After thinking more, here's a simple rule:
- For each '?', if the next character is 'D' or 'D' or '?' (i.e., if there's a possible D after it), then put 'P' to create PD
- Otherwise, put 'D'

Wait, that might not be optimal. Let me reconsider with examples.

Example 1: PD?D??P
- Position 2: T[2] = '?', T[3] = 'D' → put 'P' to get PD
- Position 3: T[3] = 'D' → fixed
- Position 4: T[4] = '?' → T[5] = '?' → next is '?' → should I put P or D?
- Position 5: T[5] = '?' → T[6] = 'P' → next is 'P' → should I put D?

For position 4: if I put 'P', I get PD if T[5] is 'D'. If I put 'D', I get 1 point directly.
For position 5: if I put 'P', I get PD if T[6] is 'D', but T[6] is 'P', so no PD. If I put 'D', I get 1 point directly.

So if I put 'P' at position 4 and 'D' at position 5:
- Position 4: P, position 5: D → PD at (4,5)
- Position 5: D → single D
- Total: PD at (4,5), D at position 5 → 2 PDs? 

Let me recalculate: 
- PD at (4,5) → 1
- D at position 5 → 1
- Total = 2

If I put 'D' at position 4 and 'P' at position 5:
- D at 4 → 1
- P at 5 → no PD (since T[6] = P)
- Total = 1

So clearly putting 'P' at 4 and 'D' at 5 is better.

For position 4: putting 'P' gives potential for PD with next character if that's 'D'
For position 5: if I put 'D', I get a direct D

But putting 'P' at 4 and 'D' at 5 gives us both a PD and a D.

Actually, the net gain: 
- Putting 'P' at 4: if I get PD, that's +1, and if I don't, 0
- Putting 'D' at 5: +1

I need to maximize the total.

This suggests a dynamic programming solution, but given the constraints (2e5), I need something efficient.

Actually, I can think about it differently: each PD pair contributes 1, each single D contributes 1. So total = count of D + count of PD.

The count of PD is equal to the number of positions i where T[i]='P' and T[i+1]='D'.

So total = (total D count) + (number of PD pairs)

I can count the fixed Ds first, and then decide for ? positions.

For each '?' position i:
- If I put 'P': I get 1 for PD if T[i+1] is 'D'
- If I put 'D': I get 1 for single D

So for each '?' at i:
- Option 1 (P): gain = 1 if T[i+1] is 'D' (and if T[i+1] is '?', I might get it later)
- Option 2 (D): gain = 1 (for the D)

But if T[i+1] is '?', I can't commit yet.

However, if T[i+1] is 'D', then putting 'P' at i gives me PD, so gain is 1 (the PD), while putting 'D' at i gives me 1 (the D). So same gain.

In this case, both give 1, so I can choose arbitrarily.

If T[i+1] is 'P', then putting 'P' at i gives no PD (since next is P), so gain is 0, while putting 'D' gives 1. So I should put 'D'.

If T[i+1] is '?', then I need to consider further.

But if T[i+1] is '?', I might get PD if I put 'P' at i and 'D' at i+1.

However, if I put 'P' at i, I get PD only if i+1 becomes 'D'. If I put 'D' at i, I get 1 point now.

So the net gain of putting 'P' at i when i+1 is '?' is 1 (for PD if i+1 becomes 'D'), while putting 'D' gives me 1 (for the D).

So they are equal in terms of point gain at that decision.

But if I put 'P' at i, I also enable the possibility of PD with i+1.

In fact, if I put 'P' at i when i+1 is '?', I get the benefit of potentially creating a PD with the future decision at i+1.

Similarly, if I put 'D' at i, I get 1 point immediately.

So both seem equivalent in terms of total potential.

But wait, if I put 'P' at i and later put 'D' at i+1, I get PD (counted in the PD pairs).
If I put 'D' at i and put 'D' at i+1, I get two Ds (counted as two single Ds).

So in terms of total quotient:
- Putting 'P' at i and 'D' at i+1: 1 PD + 0 single D at i (since P) + 1 single D at i+1 = 2
- Putting 'D' at i and 'D' at i+1: 0 PD + 1 single D at i + 1 single D at i+1 = 2

Same result!

If I put 'P' at i and 'P' at i+1: 0 PD + 0 D at i + 0 D at i+1 = 0
If I put 'D' at i and 'P' at i+1: 0 PD + 1 D at i + 0 D at i+1 = 1

So putting 'P' at i and 'D' at i+1 gives the maximum 2, same as putting 'D' at i and 'D' at i+1.

But putting 'D' at i and 'D' at i+1 also gives 2, so both are optimal.

Therefore, for a '?' followed by '?', both choices give the same total quotient.

However, if I have a choice, I can choose one that simplifies things, like putting 'P' when followed by '?' to potentially get PD.

- Position 4: P → gives PD with D at 5  
- Position 5: D → single D

This seems optimal.

I think the greedy rule is:
- For each '?' at position i:
  - If T[i+1] is 'D' (fixed), put 'P' (to form PD)

For simplicity, I'll choose 'P' when followed by '?' or 'D', and 'D' when followed by 'P'.

## Implementation

```python
T = input().strip()
result = list(T)

for i in range(len(T)):
    if result[i] == '?':
        if i + 1 < len(T):
            if result[i + 1] == 'D':
                result[i] = 'P'
            else:  # result[i+1] is 'P' or '?'
                result[i] = 'D'
        else:
            # i+1 is out of bounds
            result[i] = 'D'

print(''.join(result))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

For a string S consisting of the uppercase English letters `P` and `D`, let the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S be the total number of occurrences of `D` and `PD` in S as contiguous substrings. For example, if S = `PPDDP`, it contains two occurrences of `D` and one occurrence of `PD` as contiguous substrings, so the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S is 3.

We have a string T consisting of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`, find one with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient.

Constraints

* 1 \leq |T| \leq 2 \times 10^5
* T consists of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


T


Output

Print one string with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`. If there are multiple such strings, you may print any of them.

Examples

Input

PD?D??P


Output

PDPDPDP


Input

P?P?


Output

PDPD

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string T with characters P, D, and ?
2. We need to replace each ? with either P or D to maximize the "doctoral and postdoctoral quotient"
3. The quotient is the total count of:
   - Individual 'D' characters
   - "PD" substrings (contiguous)

## Key Insight

The quotient counts:
- Each 'D' contributes 1 point
- Each "PD" pattern contributes 1 point

So we want to maximize the sum of:
- Count of 'D' characters
- Count of "PD" substrings

## Strategy Analysis

Let's think about when "PD" appears:
- "PD" appears when we have P immediately followed by D
- To get "PD", we need a P followed by a D

Consider the contribution of each character position:
- A single D contributes 1 to the quotient
- A P followed by D contributes 1 to the quotient (for "PD") and also the D contributes 1 (so total 2)
- A P followed by P contributes 0
- A D followed by anything contributes only 1 (for the D itself)

## Greedy Approach

The key insight is that we want to create as many "PD" patterns as possible. 

For each position i:
- If we have a fixed character at position i, we can't change it
- If we have a '?', we need to decide whether to put P or D

Let's think about the optimal choice:
- If we have a P at position i, then putting D at position i+1 will create a "PD" pattern
- If we have a D at position i, it will contribute 1 to the quotient (just as a single D)
- If we have a P at position i, putting P at i+1 gives nothing useful
- If we have a D at position i, putting D at i+1 gives just the D at i+1

So we should prefer to create "PD" patterns when possible.

## Algorithm

1. We can use a greedy approach from left to right
2. For each '?', we decide based on what comes next and what comes before
3. A better strategy: try to create "PD" patterns when we have a P followed by a D

Actually, let's think differently. The quotient can be written as:
- Sum of 1 for each 'D' + Sum of 1 for each "PD"

For each position i:

## Final Algorithm

```python
T = input().strip()
n = len(T)
result = list(T)

for i in range(n):
    if T[i] != '?':
        continue
    
    # Check if we should put P or D
    # If next character is D, put P to create "PD"
    if i + 1 < n and T[i + 1] == 'D':
        result[i] = 'P'
    # If previous character is P, put D to create "PD" with previous
    elif i - 1 >= 0 and T[i - 1] == 'P':
        result[i] = 'D'
    # Otherwise, default to P
    else:
        result[i] = 'P'

print(''.join(result))
```

- We get one D at i

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

For a string S consisting of the uppercase English letters `P` and `D`, let the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S be the total number of occurrences of `D` and `PD` in S as contiguous substrings. For example, if S = `PPDDP`, it contains two occurrences of `D` and one occurrence of `PD` as contiguous substrings, so the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S is 3.

We have a string T consisting of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`, find one with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient.

Constraints

* 1 \leq |T| \leq 2 \times 10^5
* T consists of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


T


Output

Print one string with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`. If there are multiple such strings, you may print any of them.

Examples

Input

PD?D??P


Output

PDPDPDP


Input

P?P?


Output

PDPD

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

So in code:

```
result = list(T)
n = len(T)
for i in range(n):
    if T[i] == '?':
        # if next is 'D', then we have a choice
        if i+1 < n and T[i+1] == 'D':
            # if previous is 'P', choose 'D' (value 2) else choose 'P' (value 1)
            if i-1 >= 0 and result[i-1] == 'P':
                result[i] = 'D'
            else:
                result[i] = 'P'
        else:
            # next is not 'D' (could be 'P' or out of bound)
            result[i] = 'D'
```

- for each '?' at position i:

Therefore, I'll implement this. 

Note: when i-1 or i+1 is '?', we only check if it is 'D' (fixed 'D').

```python
T = input().strip()
n = len(T)
result = list(T)

for i in range(n):
    if T[i] == '?':
        # if the previous character is 'D', choose 'P'
        if i-1 >= 0 and result[i-1] == 'D':
            result[i] = 'P'
        # else if the next character is 'D', choose 'P'
        elif i+1 < n and result[i+1] == 'D':
            result[i] = 'P'
        # otherwise, choose 'D'
        else:
            result[i] = 'D'

print(''.join(result))
``` 

Let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: PD?D??P
- i=2: i-1=1='D' → choose 'P'
- i=3: i-1=2='P' (not 'D'), i+1=4='?' → not 'D' (since '?' is not 'D') → choose 'D'
- i=4: i-1=3='D' → choose 'P'
- i=5: i-1=4='P' (not 'D'), i+1=6='P' (not 'D') → choose 'D'
- So P D P D P D P → PDPDPDP ✓

Example 2: P?P?
- i=1: i-1=0='P'≠'D', i+1=2='P'→ not 'D' → choose 'D'
- i=3: i-1=2='P'≠'D', i+1= out of bound → choose 'D'
- So P D P D ✓

Perfect. The rule works. The only edge is that when i+1 is '?', we don't consider it as 'D', which is correct because we only care about fixed 'D' characters.

So the final code is:

```python
T = input().strip()
n = len(T)
result = list(T)

for i in range(n):
    if T[i] == '?':
        # if the previous character is 'D', choose 'P'
        if i-1 >= 0 and result[i-1] == 'D':
            result[i] = 'P'
        # else if the next character is 'D', choose 'P'
        elif i+1 < n and result[i+1] == 'D':
            result[i] = 'P'
        # otherwise, choose 'D'
        else:
            result[i] = 'D'

print(''.join(result))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

For a string S consisting of the uppercase English letters `P` and `D`, let the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S be the total number of occurrences of `D` and `PD` in S as contiguous substrings. For example, if S = `PPDDP`, it contains two occurrences of `D` and one occurrence of `PD` as contiguous substrings, so the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S is 3.

We have a string T consisting of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`, find one with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient.

Constraints

* 1 \leq |T| \leq 2 \times 10^5
* T consists of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


T


Output

1. We have a string T with characters 'P', 'D', and '?'
2. We need to replace each '?' with either 'P' or 'D' to maximize the "doctoral and postdoctoral quotient"

   - Single 'D' characters

## Key Insight

Let me think about when adding a 'D' or 'P' gives us more value:

1. A single 'D' contributes 1 to the quotient
2. A "PD" pair contributes 1 to the quotient (when 'P' is immediately followed by 'D')

So each position can contribute based on what we put there and what comes before it.

## Strategy Analysis

For each position i (where we have a '?'), we need to decide whether to put 'P' or 'D'.

Let's consider the contribution of each choice:

- If we put 'P' at position i:
  - It contributes 1 if the previous character is 'D' (forms "DP" → actually "PD" when reversed, so if previous is 'D', we have "DP" which is not "PD")
  - Actually, "PD" means P followed by D, so if position i-1 is 'P' and position i is 'D', we get a "PD"
  - So putting 'P' at position i contributes 1 if position i-1 is 'P' and we put 'D' at position i
  - Putting 'P' at position i also contributes 1 if position i is 'P' (this isn't right)

Wait, let me reconsider:

The quotient counts:
- Every occurrence of character 'D' separately
- Every occurrence of substring "PD"

So:
- A 'D' at position i contributes 1 (regardless of what comes before/after)
- A "PD" substring means character at i is 'P' and character at i+1 is 'D'

Therefore, for position i (0-indexed):
- If we put 'D' at position i: we get +1 (for the single D)
- If we put 'P' at position i: we get +1 if the next character (i+1) is 'D' (for the PD substring)
- Additionally, if we put 'D' at position i, we also get +1 from the D itself

```python
T = input().strip()
result = []
for i, char in enumerate(T):
    if char in 'PD':
        result.append(char)
    else:  # char is '?'
        if i == 0 or result[i-1] != 'P':
            result.append('P')
        else:
            result.append('D')

print(''.join(result))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

For a string S consisting of the uppercase English letters `P` and `D`, let the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S be the total number of occurrences of `D` and `PD` in S as contiguous substrings. For example, if S = `PPDDP`, it contains two occurrences of `D` and one occurrence of `PD` as contiguous substrings, so the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S is 3.

We have a string T consisting of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`, find one with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient.

Constraints

* 1 \leq |T| \leq 2 \times 10^5
* T consists of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


T


Output

Print one string with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`. If there are multiple such strings, you may print any of them.

Examples

Input

PD?D??P


Output

PDPDPDP


Input

P?P?


Output

PDPD

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string T with characters 'P', 'D', and '?'
2. We need to replace each '?' with either 'P' or 'D' to maximize the "doctoral and postdoctoral quotient"
3. The quotient is the total count of:
   - Occurrences of 'D' as a single character
   - Occurrences of 'PD' as a contiguous substring

## Key Insights

Let me think about what contributes to the quotient:
- Every 'D' character contributes 1 to the quotient
- Every "PD" substring contributes 1 to the quotient

So we want to maximize the total count of D characters and PD substrings.

## Strategy Analysis

For each position, we need to decide whether to put 'P' or 'D' in place of '?'.

Let's consider the contribution of each position:
- If we place 'D' at position i: it contributes 1 (for being D) + 1 if position i-1 is 'P' (for forming "PD" with previous character)
- If we place 'P' at position i: it contributes 0 (no D) + 1 if position i-1 is 'D' (for forming "PD" with previous character)

Wait, let me reconsider the "PD" substring. "PD" means P followed by D, so:
- A "PD" pattern occurs when we have 'P' at position i and 'D' at position i+1
- A single 'D' contributes when we have just 'D'

Actually, let me be more precise:
- A single 'D' contributes 1 at every position where we have 'D'
- A substring "PD" contributes 1 when we have 'P' followed by 'D'

So for each pair of adjacent positions (i, i+1), if we have 'P' at i and 'D' at i+1, we get +1.
For each single position i, if we have 'D' at i, we get +1.

## Greedy Approach

Let me think about this optimally. For each '?', we need to decide whether to put 'P' or 'D'.

Consider two adjacent positions where one is fixed and one is '?':
- If we have 'P' at position i and '?' at position i+1:
  - If we put 'D' at i+1: we get +1 for the D at i+1, and +1 for the "PD" at positions (i,i+1)
  - If we put 'P' at i+1: we get nothing
  - So put 'D' at i+1

- If we have '?' at position i and 'D' at position i+1:
  - If we put 'P' at i: we get +1 for "PD" at positions (i,i+1)
  - If we put 'D' at i: we get +1 for D at i, but no "PD" at (i,i+1)
  - We get more when we put 'P' at i (since we get the "PD" bonus of 1)

- If we have '?' at position i and '?' at position i+1:
  - We need to maximize both the D count and PD count
  - If we put 'P' at i and 'D' at i+1: we get +1 for "PD" at (i,i+1)
  - If we put 'D' at i and 'P' at i+1: we get +1 for D at i
  - If we put 'D' at i and 'D' at i+1: we get +2 for D's
  - If we put 'P' at i and 'P' at i+1: we get nothing
  - The choice depends on context, but we generally want to create "PD" patterns

- Input: PD?D??P
- Output: PDPDPDP

- If we have a 'P' to its left (i-1), putting 'D' here would create a "PD" with the P at i-1

But actually, putting 'D' when there's a 'P' to its left creates "PD" (P at i-1, D at i), and putting 'P' when there's a 'D' to its right creates "PD" (P at i, D at i+1).

So the optimal strategy seems to be:
- For each '?', if there's a 'D' immediately to its right, and we don't have a 'P' to its left, prefer 'P' to create a "PD" with the right D
- If there's a 'P' immediately to its left, and we don't have a 'D' to its right, prefer 'D' to create a "PD" with the left P

- For each '?', if the character to its right is 'D', then put 'P' here (to create "PD")

  - We get +1 if position i+1 is 'D' (for "PD" at i,i+1)

  - We get +1 (for the D itself)
  - We get +1 if position i-1 is 'P' (for "PD" at i-1,i)

- 'P' gives: (1 if left is D else 0) + (1 if right is D else 0)
- 'D' gives: 1 + (1 if left is P else 0)

We choose whichever gives higher value.

Alternative approach: Dynamic programming or observation.

Let me think about what the optimal pattern looks like.

From the examples:
- PD?D??P → PDPDPDP (alternating P,D starting with P)

In fact, a pattern that maximizes PD and D occurrences might be alternating.

Actually, let me think about the total contribution:
- Every PD pattern contributes 1
- Every D contributes 1
- Total = (number of D's) + (number of PD pairs)

The number of PD pairs is the sum over i of [1 if T[i]=='P' and T[i+1]=='D']

So total = sum over i of [T[i]=='D'] + sum over i of [T[i]=='P' and T[i+1]=='D']

We can rewrite this as: sum over i of [T[i]=='D'] + sum over i of [T[i]=='P' and T[i+1]=='D']

This equals: sum over i of [T[i]=='D'] + sum over i of [T[i]=='P' and T[i+1]=='D']

I think the optimal solution is to create as many PD patterns as possible. A PD pattern is P followed by D.

So we want to create P->D transitions.

For each '?', if we can create a transition to a D to its right, or from a P to its left, we should do it.

After reconsidering, I believe the intended solution is:

For each '?', if there's a 'D' immediately to its right, put 'P' here to create a PD pair.
Otherwise, if there's a 'P' immediately to its left, put 'D' here to create a PD pair.
Otherwise, put 'P' (or D - doesn't matter).

This greedy approach works because:
- Putting 'P' when right is 'D' creates a PD pair with the right D
- Putting 'D' when left is 'P' creates a PD pair with the left P

Let me test with PD?D??P:
- i=0: P → keep
- i=1: D → keep
- i=2: right is D → put P → now P,D,P,D,?,?
- i=3: D → keep
- i=4: right is ? → no D, left is D → not P, so put P → now P,D,P,D,P,?,?
- i=5: right is P → no D, left is P → put D → now P,D,P,D,P,D,P

Result: PDPDPDP

This matches the expected output!

Let me verify the second example: P?P?
- i=0: P → keep
- i=1: right is P → no D, left is P → put D → now P,D,P,?
- i=2: P → keep
- i=3: right is none, left is P → put D → now P,D,P,D

Result: PDPD, which matches.

So the algorithm is:
For each position i in T:
- If T[i] is not '?', keep it
- If T[i] is '?':
  - If T[i+1] == 'D' (and i+1 < len(T)), then put 'P'
  - Else if T[i-1] == 'P' (and i-1 >= 0), then put 'D'
  - Else, put 'P' (default)

```python
T = input().strip()
n = len(T)
result = list(T)

for i in range(n):
    if T[i] != '?':
        continue
    
    # Check if we can create PD by having P followed by D
    # If there's a D immediately to the right, put P
    if i + 1 < n and T[i + 1] == 'D':
        result[i] = 'P'
    # Else, if there's a P immediately to the left, put D (to create PD with left P)
    elif i - 1 >= 0 and T[i - 1] == 'P':
        result[i] = 'D'
    # Otherwise, put P (arbitrary choice)
    else:
        result[i] = 'P'

print(''.join(result))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

For a string S consisting of the uppercase English letters `P` and `D`, let the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S be the total number of occurrences of `D` and `PD` in S as contiguous substrings. For example, if S = `PPDDP`, it contains two occurrences of `D` and one occurrence of `PD` as contiguous substrings, so the doctoral and postdoctoral quotient of S is 3.

We have a string T consisting of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`, find one with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient.

Constraints

* 1 \leq |T| \leq 2 \times 10^5
* T consists of `P`, `D`, and `?`.

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


T


Output

Print one string with the maximum possible doctoral and postdoctoral quotient among the strings that can be obtained by replacing each `?` in T with `P` or `D`. If there are multiple such strings, you may print any of them.

Examples

Input

PD?D??P


Output

PDPDPDP


Input

P?P?


Output

PDPD

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

- Other: DPD → D's: 2, PD patterns: (1,2)? P-D → yes → total 3
- So maximum is 3.

- What if I do PDP D: D's: 2, PD patterns: (0,1)? P-D, (1,2)? D-P no, (2,3)? P-D → so (0,1) and (2,3) → 2 PD patterns → total 4
- What if I do DPD D: D's: 3, PD patterns: (1,2)? P-D → yes → total 4
- What if I do DDD D: 4 D's, 0 patterns → total 4

My rule gives DDDD with total 4, which is optimal.

Therefore, the solution is: replace every '?' with 'D'.

```python
T = input().strip()
result = ""
for char in T:
    if char == '?':
        result += 'D'
    else:
        result += char
print(result)
```

## Solution

```python
T = input().strip()
n = len(T)
result = []

for i in range(n):
    if T[i] != '?':
        result.append(T[i])
    else:
        # If this is not the last character and next character is D or ?
        if i < n - 1 and T[i + 1] == 'D' or T[i + 1] == '?':
            result.append('P')
        else:
            result.append('D')

print(''.join(result))
```

- Each 'D' contributes 1 to the quotient
- Each 'PD' contributes 1 to the quotient

Algorithm:

   - If T[i] is '?', and T[i+1] is fixed as 'D' (or I can set it to 'D' later), then I can get a PD pattern by putting P at i.
   - But I also get a single D from putting D at i.

2. Actually, both choices give me at least 1 contribution, but putting P at i requires that i+1 is D (or I set it to D).

3. If I set i+1 to D (by putting P at i), I get a PD pattern, which contributes 1.
4. If I set i to D, I get 1 from the single D.

Since the contribution is the same, I should prefer to put P if it allows me to create a PD pattern that might be useful for the next position, or if it helps maintain a pattern.

But looking at the output: PDPDPDP for PD?D??P.

- T = PD?D??P
- Result = PDPDPDP

- PD at positions 0-1, 2-3, 4-5, 6-7? Wait, length is 7.

D's at positions 1, 3, 5 → 3 D's
Total = 3 + 3 = 6

- D's: positions 1,2,3,4 → 4
- PD patterns: only position 5-6? No, 5-6 is DP, not PD. 4-5 is DD, not PD. So PD patterns: none? 
- Actually 0-1 is PD, 1-2 is DD, etc. Only 0-1 is PD → 1 PD
- Total = 4 + 1 = 5 < 6

So PDPDPDP is better.

The key insight: alternating P and D maximizes the pattern PD and the single D.

In fact, since PD contributes 1 and single D contributes 1, and alternating gives both, it seems optimal.

Moreover, in the alternating pattern, every pair (P,D) gives both a PD pattern and a D.

So for consecutive positions, if I alternate, I get exactly one PD pattern and one D for every two positions.

But let me verify the contribution:
- For a sequence "PD": 
  - D at position 1: +1

  - Total: 0

So by alternating, I create many PD patterns and many D's.

In fact, in an alternating string starting with P: PDPD... 
- Every D contributes 1
- Every PD pattern (P followed by D) contributes 1
- Total = (number of D's) + (number of PD patterns) = (n/2) + (n/2 - 1) = n-1 for even n, or similar.

In "PDP": 
- D's: 1 (at position 1)
- PD patterns: 0-1 (P at 0, D at 1) → 1
- Total: 1 + 1 = 2
- n=3, so 3-1=2 ✓

In "PD": 
- D's: 1

- Total: 2

So alternating seems better.

But is alternating always optimal?

Consider when we have fixed characters. We need to preserve fixed characters.

For each '?', I need to decide between P and D.

The contribution when I put P at position i:
- I get 1 if position i+1 is D (or can be set to D)
- I don't get anything else from position i

The contribution when I put D at position i:
- I get 1 from the single D

So if position i+1 is not D and not '?', then putting P gives 0, putting D gives 1. So I should put D.

If position i+1 is 'D' or '?', then both options give me at least 1.

In the case where i+1 is '?', putting P forces i+1 to be D, which allows me to get a PD pattern at i, and also creates a D at i+1.

But if I put D at i, I get a D at i, and i+1 can be set to P or D later.

There's a trade-off.

Actually, if I put D at i and i+1 is ?, I get 1 from D at i, and i+1 is free.
If I put P at i and i+1 is ?, I get 1 from PD (if i+1 is D), and i+1 must be D.

Since both give me 1 in contribution, I don't have a clear preference, but I should consider the future.

However, the problem asks for any string with maximum quotient, not necessarily unique.

Looking at the examples:
- PD?D??P → PDPDPDP
- P?P? → PDPD

In P?P?: 
- Position 1: ? 
- Position 3: ?

If I choose D, I don't force anything.

In fact, for any ? at position i, if i+1 is not 'D', then putting P gives 0, putting D gives 1. So I should put D.

- Putting P gives 1 (from PD pattern)

- Putting P forces i+1 to be D, giving me 1 from PD
- Putting D gives me 1 from single D, and i+1 can be anything

So both give 1.

Therefore, I have freedom. I can choose arbitrarily.

In the first example: PD?D??P
- First ? at position 2: T[3] is 'D' → both choices give 1
- Second ? at position 4: T[5] is '?' → both give 1
- Third ? at position 5: T[6] is 'P' → cannot form PD pattern, so if I put P at 5, I get 0, if I put D at 5, I get 1 → so I should put D
